Figure 1.
Structure of the paper

The rest of the paper is dedicated to proving Theorem
1.1
. In Section
5.2
, we reduce the proof of Theorem
1.1
to Lemma
5.3
after an application of Proposition
5.1
, which is essentially a restatement of Theorem
1.2
. We also recall some known geometric properties of a non-singular complete intersection variety defined by two quadrics which provide guidelines for our estimations for the minor arc contributions. Section
6
is dedicated to the exponential integral bounds.
We begin by re-interpreting known bounds for quadratic exponential integrals to our setting and finish with a key result Lemma
6.4
, which plays a critical role in carrying out a
double
Kloosterman refinement in the case
s=9
. Section
7
is dedicated to the exponential sum estimates, most of which are either known or are direct analogues of their known function field counterparts. An asymptotic formula for the major arcs contribution is obtained in Section
8
. After the preparations in Section
9
, we prove sufficient bounds for the minor arcs contributions coming from the
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
functions in Section
10
and Sections
11
-
13
. See Figure
1
for the connections between these sections. While the final optimization in the extreme cases follows closely that in
[
37
]
, the growth of our functions, for instance in (
1.7
), requires special care in non-extreme cases (small/medium
q
and
{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}
).

2.
Basic notation and
h
-function

We first begin by setting notation that will be throughout. We also draw the reader’s attention to Section
5
, where much additional terminology is introduced which is used from that point on, and to Section
11.1
, where the expressions
\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}
and
\Sigma(\cdots)
are defined.

The notation
|\,\cdot\,|
,
\delta_{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}
,
A\ll B
,
A\asymp B
,
e(x)
,
e_{q}(x)
,
\sum_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\>\text{mod}\>q}^{\ast}
and
\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)
will always be as defined before Theorem
1.2
.
We use
\vec{\mathrm{a}},\vec{\mathrm{b}},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\vec{\mathrm{r}}
to denote vectors in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{u},\dots
to denote vectors in
\mathbb{Z}^{s}
, where
s
will denote the number of variables needed to define the quadratic forms appearing in the statement of Theorem
1.1
.
Set
\mathds{1}_{S}=1
if
S
is true and

0
otherwise. In order to reserve the symbol
\cdot
for the scalar product of two vectors, we use
\times
where necessary to denote multiplication in a field.

Recall that we fix smooth functions
\omega
and
\omega_{0}
on
\mathbb{R}
as described before Theorem
1.2
.
Additionally, from Section
5.2
onward, we fix a weight
w\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{s})
, fix a pair of quadratic forms
F_{1},F_{2}
in
s
variables, and use
\varepsilon
to represent an arbitrarily small positive constant whose value may vary from line to line. All implicit constants in
\ll,O(\,\cdot\,)
and
\asymp
notation are then allowed to depend on
s,w,F_{1},F_{2}
and
\varepsilon
.

We define

(2.1)

c=(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega(|{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}|)\,d{\vec{\mathrm{x}}})^{-1}=(2\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}}r\omega(r)\,dr)^{-1}.

Throughout this paper
c
always denotes this particular constant in (
2.1
).

For each 2-vector
{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}=(x,y)
we write
{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}^{\perp}=(y,-x)
. We write
\partial_{\vec{\xi}}=\frac{\xi_{1}}{|\vec{\xi}|}\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{1}}+\frac{\xi_{2}}{|\vec{\xi}|}\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{2}}
to denote the normalized directional derivative (with respect to
{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}
) along
\vec{\xi}
.

Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a lattice, where we will typically take
\Lambda=\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)
. We shall study the shortest non-zero vector of the lattice
M\Lambda=\{M{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}:{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}\in\Lambda\}
. More precisely, if
M
is an
m\times 2
real matrix with full rank, we define

\mu_{M}:=\mu_{M}(\Lambda)=\min\{|M{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}|:{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}\in\Lambda\setminus\{\vec{0}\}\}.

In other words
\mu_{M}(\Lambda)
is the Euclidean norm of the shortest nonzero vector of the lattice
M\Lambda
.

Given a function
\omega_{1}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with support on
(1/2,1)
, we define a function

(2.2)

h_{\omega_{1}}(y,z)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{yj}\left(\omega_{1}(yj)-\omega_{1}\left(\frac{|z|}{yj}\right)\right).

We will usually take
\omega_{1}=\omega
, and so we write
h(y,z)
as an abbreviation of
h_{\omega}(y,z)
. This function
appears in the one dimensional version of the delta symbol method in
[
14
]
and will appear in the definition of
p_{\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}
.
However we use
\omega_{1}(x)=x\omega(x)
in the proof of Lemma
4.6
, where we write
h_{2}(y,z)=h_{x\omega(x)}(y,z)
.

We need a variant of
[
14
, Lemmas 4 and 9]
on the properties of the
h
-function.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\omega_{1}
be a
smooth function supported on
(1/2,1)
. Recall the function
h_{\omega_{1}}(y,z)
from (
2.2
).

(1)

The value of
h_{\omega_{1}}(y,z)
is non-zero only if
y\leq\max(1,2|z|)
. Moreover
h_{\omega_{1}}(y,z)\ll_{\omega_{1}}1/y
for all
z
.

(2)

Suppose that
A,B,\delta>0
and that
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies
|f^{(k)}(z)|\leq C_{k}A^{k}
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}
and some sequence of positive real numbers
\bm{C}=(C_{k})_{k\geq 0}
.
Then for
0<y\leq Q^{-\delta}\min\{B/A,1\}
we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(z)h_{\omega_{1}}(y,Bz)\,dz=B^{-1}f(0)\int_{\mathbb{R}}\omega_{1}(z)\,dz+O_{N,\delta,\bm{C},\omega_{1}}(B^{-1}Q^{-N}).

Proof.

We use the results from Heath-Brown
[
14
, Section 4]
with
\omega_{1}
in place of the function
\omega
appearing there. This deserves a word of justification, as Heath-Brown states his results for a specific function
\omega
which he constructs explicitly. However, it only matters that this function is fixed, nonnegative, infinitely differentiable (hence in particular with bounded derivatives of all orders),
L^{1}
-normalized, and supported on
(1/2,1)
. One can inspect the proofs and see that only bounds for some finitely many derivatives of
\omega
enter into the error terms; one can also consult the comments prior to
[
14
, Lemma 6]
where it is mentioned that any other choice for
\omega
would work as well. Furthermore, the conclusions of our lemma are invariant if we multiply
\omega_{1}
by a constant. Thus for
L^{1}
-normalization, we can assume that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\omega_{1}(z)\,dz=1
, as required. The other properties already hold, and the implicit constants in the lemma in the lemma are permitted (as they must be) to depend on the choice of function
\omega_{1}
.

The first part of the lemma follows at once from
[
14
, Lemma 4]
. The rest of the proof concerns the second part of the lemma.

Making a change of variable
z^{\prime}=z/B
, it is enough to consider the case when
B=1
.
Note that for any
R\in\mathbb{N}
,
[
14
, Lemma 5]
gives

\int_{|z|>yQ^{\delta/2}}f(z)h_{\omega_{1}}(y,z)\,dz\ll_{R}C_{0}\int_{|z|>yQ^{\delta/2}}y^{-1}(y^{R}+(y/|z|)^{R})\,dz,

which is
O_{N,\delta,\bm{C}}(Q^{-N})
if we choose
R
sufficiently large when
y\leq Q^{-\delta}
. It remains to show

(2.3)

\int_{-yQ^{\delta/2}}^{yQ^{\delta/2}}f(z)h_{\omega_{1}}(y,z)\,dz=f(0)+O_{N,\delta,\bm{C}}(Q^{-N}).

Using the Taylor expansion

f(t)=f(0)+\sum_{n=1}^{2M}f_{n}t^{n}+O_{\bm{C}}((At)^{2M+1}),

we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{-yQ^{\delta/2}}^{yQ^{\delta/2}}f(z)h_{\omega_{1}}(y,z)\,dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{2M}f_{n}\int_{-yQ^{\delta/2}}^{yQ^{\delta/2}}z^{n}h_{\omega_{1}}(y,z)\,dz+O_{\bm{C}}\left(f_{2M+1}\int_{-yQ^{\delta/2}}^{yQ^{\delta/2}}|z|^{2M+1}y^{-1}\,dz\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle f(0)+O_{R,\bm{C}}\bigg(\sum_{n=0}^{2M}(AyQ^{\delta/2})^{n}\big(y^{R}Q^{\delta/2}+Q^{-R\delta/2}\big)+\frac{A^{2M+1}(yQ^{\delta/2})^{2M+2}}{y}\bigg),

by
[
14
, Lemmas 6, 8]
and the assumption
f_{n}\ll_{n}A^{n}C_{n}
.
By the condition on the size of
y
, this becomes

\displaystyle f(0)+O_{R,\bm{C}}\bigg(\sum_{n=0}^{2M}(Q^{-\delta/2})^{n}(Q^{-R\delta+\delta/2}+Q^{-R\delta/2})+(Q^{-\delta/2})^{2M+1}Q^{\delta/2}\bigg),

and (
2.3
) follows.
∎

3.
Two-dimensional delta symbol: duality and
p
-functions

In this section, we make some preparations to prove Theorem
1.2
.
We begin by elaborating the first and key step that facilitates the two-dimensional delta symbol in Lemma
3.1
below. This can be seen as a higher dimensional analogue of the equality used in the one dimensional delta symbol method of Duke–Friedlander–Iwaniec
[
11
, (2.3)]
.

3.1.
Detecting the delta symbol by duality of divisors

We observe that if
\vec{\mathrm{n}}\not=\vec{0}
, then there is a unique primitive vector
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
such that
\vec{\mathrm{n}}=\lambda{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{\perp}
for some
\lambda>0
. By symmetry, the sets

\{d:d\mid\lambda\}
and
\{\lambda/d:d\mid\lambda\}
are the same as long as
\lambda>0
. The vector
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
can be determined using the condition
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}}=0
, which can be detected with the one dimensional delta symbol. It remains to determine the size of
d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
that we shall use.
The two-dimensional Dirichlet approximation states that, given any real numbers
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
and a natural number
Q
, there exist integers
a_{1},a_{2}
and
1\leq q\leq Q
such that

(3.1)

\Big|\alpha_{i}-\frac{a_{i}}{q}\Big|\leq\frac{1}{qQ^{1/2}},\quad i=1,2.

We would like to choose
Q^{1+1/2}\asymp|\vec{\mathrm{n}}|
so that the error terms of the approximation do not oscillate when
q\asymp Q
.
The size of the
d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
parameters will be chosen so that the support of the resulting functions roughly matches (
3.1
).

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\omega(x),

h(y,z),
and
c
be as in Section
2
.
Let
Q\geq 1
be a parameter and let
\vec{\mathrm{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that
|\vec{\mathrm{n}}|\leq\frac{1}{2}Q^{3/2}
. Then for any
N>0
, we have

(3.2)

\displaystyle\delta_{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{Q^{3}}\sum_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\omega\Big(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\Big)\sum_{q\leq Q}\,\,\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod q\\
q\mid\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\perp}\end{subarray}}e_{q}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})h\Big(\frac{\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}})q}{\gcd(q,\vec{\mathrm{r}})Q},\frac{\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{Q^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{2c}{Q}\sum_{d^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}}\mathds{1}_{d^{\prime}\mid\vec{\mathrm{n}}}\omega\Big(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{n}}|}{d^{\prime}Q^{1/2}}\Big)+O_{N}(Q^{-N}).

Proof.

If
\vec{\mathrm{n}}\not=\vec{0}
, then there exist
exactly two
primitive vectors
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
such that
\vec{\mathrm{n}}=\lambda{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{\perp}
for some
\lambda\neq 0
. Therefore, by writing
\vec{\mathrm{n}}=dd^{\prime}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{\perp}
, whenever
\vec{\mathrm{n}}\not=\vec{0}
, we have

(3.3)

\displaystyle\sum_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}\delta_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}}}\sum_{d\mid\vec{\mathrm{n}}}\omega\Big(\frac{d|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|}{Q_{1}}\Big)-2\sum_{d^{\prime}\mid\vec{\mathrm{n}}}\omega\Big(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{n}}|}{d^{\prime}Q_{1}}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}\sum_{d\mid\vec{\mathrm{n}}}\delta_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\frac{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{d}}\omega\Big(\frac{d|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|}{Q_{1}}\Big)-2\sum_{d^{\prime}\mid\vec{\mathrm{n}}}\omega\Big(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{n}}|}{d^{\prime}Q_{1}}\Big)=0,

where
Q_{1}
is some parameter to be chosen later and the sum over
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is over primitive integer vectors.

We now apply the one dimensional delta symbol
[
14
, Theorem 1]
with parameter
Q_{2}
to detect
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\frac{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{d}=0
. We may allow
Q_{2}
to depend on
d
and
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
; we can write this as
Q_{2}=Q_{2}(d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})
without loss of generality, since
d=\gcd(d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})
.
This gives us

(3.4)

\displaystyle\delta_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\frac{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{d}}=\frac{c_{Q_{2}}}{Q_{2}^{2}}\sum_{q}\ \sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{a\bmod q}e_{q}(a{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\frac{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{d})h\Big(\frac{q}{Q_{2}},\frac{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{dQ_{2}^{2}}\Big),

where
h
is defined below (
2.2
) and

c_{Q_{2}}=1+O_{N}(Q_{2}^{-N})
for any
N
. Using the support of
h(y,z)
as recorded in Lemma
2.1
, together with the assumption on the size of
\vec{\mathrm{n}}
, we can only have non-zero contributions from terms satisfying

(3.5)

q\leq\max\{Q_{2},{Q_{1}d^{-2}Q^{3/2}}{Q_{2}^{-1}}\}.

Since eventually we would seek a bound of the type
qd\leq Q
, it is natural for us to choose
Q_{2}=Q/d
so that (
3.5
) becomes

q\leq\max\{Q/d,Q_{1}Q^{1/2}/d\}=Q/d,

upon further choosing
Q_{1}=Q^{1/2}
.
With the choice
Q_{2}=Q/d
and
Q_{1}=Q^{1/2}
, we see that (
3.4
) becomes

\displaystyle\delta_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\frac{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{d}}=\frac{d^{2}}{Q^{2}}\sum_{q\leq Q/d}\ \sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{a\bmod q}e_{q}(a{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\frac{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{d})h\Big(\frac{qd}{Q},\frac{d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{Q^{2}}\Big)+O_{N}(Q^{4}Q_{2}^{-N}),

for any
N>1
. As
Q^{1/2}\leq Q/Q_{1}\leq Q/d=Q_{2}
, the error term above is
O_{N}(Q^{4-N/2})
. Therefore, introducing the sum over
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
and
d
yields

\displaystyle\sum_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}\sum_{d\mid\vec{\mathrm{n}}}\delta_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\frac{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{d}}\omega\Big(\frac{d|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}\sum_{d\mid\vec{\mathrm{n}}}\omega\Big(\frac{d|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\Big)\frac{d^{2}}{Q^{2}}\sum_{q\leq Q/d}\ \sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{a\bmod q}e_{q}(a{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\frac{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{d})h\Big(\frac{qd}{Q},\frac{d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{Q^{2}}\Big)+O_{N}(Q^{5-N/2}),

for any
N>1
. After using the additive characters to replace the condition
d\mid\vec{\mathrm{n}}
, we obtain for any primitive
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{d\mid\vec{\mathrm{n}}}\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{a\bmod q}e_{q}(a{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\frac{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{d})=\sum_{d}\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\bmod q\\
(a,q)=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\vec{\mathrm{b}}\bmod d}e_{qd}(a{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}}+q\vec{\mathrm{b}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d}\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{qd}\\
q\mid{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\perp}\\
(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)=1\end{subarray}}e_{qd}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})=\sum_{d}\frac{1}{d^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d_{1},d_{2}\\
d=d_{1}d_{2}\\
(d_{1},q)=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod qd_{2}\\
q\mid{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot d_{1}\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\perp}\\
(\vec{\mathrm{a}},qd_{2})=1\end{subarray}}e_{qd_{2}}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}}),

upon writing
d_{1}=\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{a}},d)
and then replacing
\vec{\mathrm{a}}
by
d_{1}\vec{\mathrm{a}}
.
We then re-name
qd_{2}
as
q
and
\vec{\mathrm{r}}=d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
so that
d=\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}})
,
Q_{2}=Q_{2}(\vec{\mathrm{r}})
, and
d_{2}=\gcd(q,d)=\gcd(q,\vec{\mathrm{r}})
, to get

\displaystyle\sum_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}\sum_{d\mid\vec{\mathrm{n}}}\delta_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\frac{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{d}}\omega\Big(\frac{d|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Q^{2}}\sum_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}=d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}\omega\Big(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\Big)\sum_{q\leq Q/d_{1}}\;\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod q\\
q\mid\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\perp}\end{subarray}}e_{q}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})h\Big(\frac{qd_{1}}{Q},\frac{\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{Q^{2}}\Big)+O_{N}(Q^{5-N/2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Q^{2}}\sum_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}=d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}\omega\Big(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\Big)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\leq Q\end{subarray}}\;\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod q\\
q\mid\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\perp}\end{subarray}}e_{q}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})h\Big(\frac{\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}})q}{\gcd(q,\vec{\mathrm{r}})Q},\frac{\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{Q^{2}}\Big)+O_{N}(Q^{5-N/2}).

Here in the last equality, we have used
d_{1}=d/d_{2}=\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}})/\gcd(q,\vec{\mathrm{r}})
and relaxed the upper bound for
q
using the fact that the terms with
q>Q/d_{1}
are all zero from Lemma
2.1
.
To summarize, we have

(3.6)

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}\sum_{d\mid\vec{\mathrm{n}}}\delta_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\frac{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{d}}\omega\Big(\frac{d|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Q^{2}}\sum_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}=d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}\omega\Big(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\Big)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\leq Q\end{subarray}}\;\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod q\\
q\mid\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\perp}\end{subarray}}e_{q}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})h\Big(\frac{\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}})q}{\gcd(q,\vec{\mathrm{r}})Q},\frac{\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{Q^{2}}\Big)+O_{N}(Q^{-N}),

for any
N>1
.

For
\vec{\mathrm{n}}\not=\vec{0}
, we see that (
3.2
) follows from (
3.3
) and (
3.6
).
When
\vec{\mathrm{n}}=\vec{0}
, the left-hand side of (
3.3
) becomes

\displaystyle\sum_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}\sum_{d\mid\vec{\mathrm{n}}}\delta_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\frac{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{d}}\omega\Big(\frac{d|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\Big)=\sum_{d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}\omega\Big(\frac{d|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\Big)=c^{-1}Q+O_{N}(Q^{-N}),

for any
N>0
with
c
is defined in (
2.1
), and so by (
3.6
) we see that (
3.2
) holds in this case as well.
∎

3.2.
Defining the
p
-functions

Next, we use Fourier inversion to connect the expression on the right hand side of (
3.2
) to the
p
-functions appearing in Theorem
1.2
. The first sum in (
3.2
) can be interpreted as placing rectangular arcs around rationals
\vec{\mathrm{a}}/q
with
q\leq Q
and
\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)=1

along the line

q\mid\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\perp}
for every
|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|\asymp Q^{1/2}
, which results in the term
p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}
where
k=\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}})/\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}},q)
. The second sum in (
3.2
) corresponds to placing symmetric square arcs
around each rational
\vec{\mathrm{a}}/q
with
q\leq Q
and
\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)=1
, which gives rise to the term
p_{1,q}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\omega_{0},\omega
be as in Section
2
. Let
\vec{\mathrm{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and let
Q\geq 1
be a large parameter.
Then we have

(3.7)

\begin{split}\delta_{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}=&\sum_{1\leq q\leq Q}\underset{\hphantom{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{q}}}{\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{q}}}e_{q}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bigg(p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})\\
&\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\mid\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\perp}\\
k=\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}})/\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}},q)\end{subarray}}\omega\bigg(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\bigg)p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})\bigg)e({\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}+O_{N}(Q^{-N})\end{split}

for any
N>0
where

(3.8)

\displaystyle\quad p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})

\displaystyle=-\frac{2c}{Q}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega_{0}\left(\frac{|{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}|}{Q^{3/2}}\right)\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{q^{2}j^{2}}\omega\left(\frac{|{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}|}{jqQ^{1/2}}\right)e(-{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{x}}})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{x}}},

(3.9)

\displaystyle\quad p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{Q^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega_{0}\left(\frac{|{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}|}{Q^{3/2}}\right)h\left(\frac{kq}{Q},\frac{\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}}{Q^{2}}\right)e(-{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{x}}})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}.

Here
h(y,z)
is defined by (
2.2
).

Proof.

We will introduce a smooth weight on
\vec{\mathrm{n}}
in (
3.2
) by observing

\delta_{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}=\omega_{0}\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{n}}|}{Q^{3/2}}\right)\delta_{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}.

After using Fourier inversion in the
\vec{\mathrm{n}}
variable, we see that the first term on the right side in (
3.2
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{c}{Q^{3}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq q\leq Q\end{subarray}}\;\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod q}e_{q}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\mid\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\perp}\end{subarray}}\omega\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\right)h\left(\frac{\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}})q}{\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}},q)Q},\frac{\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{Q^{2}}\right)\omega_{0}\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{n}}|}{Q^{3/2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{Q^{3}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq q\leq Q\end{subarray}}\;\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod q}e_{q}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\mid\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\perp}\\
\mathclap{k=\frac{\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}})}{\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}},q)}}\end{subarray}}\omega\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\right)h\left(\frac{kq}{Q},\frac{\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}}}{Q^{2}}\right)\omega_{0}\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{n}}|}{Q^{3/2}}\right)

(3.10)

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq q\leq Q}\underset{\hphantom{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{q}}}{\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{q}}}e_{q}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\mid\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\perp}\\
\mathclap{k=\frac{\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}})}{\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}},q)}}\end{subarray}}\omega\bigg(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\bigg)p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})e({\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}},

where
p_{{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}}
is defined in (
3.9
).
Similarly, the second term on the right side in (
3.2
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{2c}{Q}\omega_{0}\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{n}}|}{Q^{3/2}}\right)\sum_{d^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}}\mathds{1}_{d^{\prime}\mid\vec{\mathrm{n}}}\,\omega\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{n}}|}{d^{\prime}Q^{1/2}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2c}{Q}\omega_{0}\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{n}}|}{Q^{3/2}}\right)\sum_{1\leq q\leq Q}\frac{1}{q^{2}}{\sum_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\,(q)}}^{*}e_{q}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{j^{2}}\omega\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{n}}|}{jqQ^{1/2}}\right)

(3.11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{1\leq q\leq Q}\underset{\hphantom{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{q}}}{\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{q}}}e_{q}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})e({\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}},

where
p_{1,q}
is defined as in (
3.8
).
The condition
q\leq Q
is introduced due to support conditions on
\omega_{0}
and
\omega
.
And the result follows from Lemma
3.1
, (
3.10
) and (
3.11
).
∎

3.3.
The function
p_{1,q}

We give an estimate of of
p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})
defined in (
3.8
) in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
1\leq q\leq Q
be an integer,
{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})
be as defined in (
3.8
). Then for any
N>0
,

p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})\ll_{N}\frac{Q}{q(1+|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|Q^{3/2})}(1+|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|qQ^{1/2})^{-N}.

Proof.

Making a change of variables, we write

\displaystyle p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})

\displaystyle=-2c\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega_{0}\left(\frac{jq|{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}|}{Q}\right)\omega\left(|{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}|\right)e(-qjQ^{1/2}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{x}}})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}.

Since
\omega
is supported in
(1/2,1)
and
\omega_{0}
is supported in
(-1/2,1/2)
, the sum over
j
becomes
1\leq j\ll Q/q
. We therefore reach a trivial bound

(3.12)

\displaystyle|p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})|\ll Q/q.

Alternatively, upon repeated integration by parts, for a fixed
j\ll Q/q
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega_{0}\left(\frac{jq|{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}|}{Q}\right)\omega\left(|{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}|\right)e(-qjQ^{1/2}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{x}}})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}\right|\ll_{N}(1+qjQ^{1/2}|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|)^{-N},

which leads to an alternate bound

(3.13)

|p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})|\ll_{N}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}(1+qQ^{1/2}j|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|)^{-N}\ll_{N}(qQ^{1/2}|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|)^{-1}(1+qQ^{1/2}|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|)^{-N}.

The lemma then follows by combining (
3.12
) and (
3.13
).
∎

3.4.
The function
p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}

We first give various estimates for the function
p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}
defined in (
3.9
). In particular, these estimates show that
p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}
is supported in a rectangle around the origin with side length
O(1/Q^{3/2})
in the direction of
\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}
and side length
O(1/kqQ^{1/2})
in the direction of
\vec{\mathrm{r}}
in Lemma
3.4
. We then provide an asymptotic evaluation of
p_{r,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}
for certain range of
{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}
in Lemma
3.5
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Write
\partial_{\vec{\xi}}=\frac{\xi_{1}}{|\vec{\xi}|}\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{1}}+\frac{\xi_{2}}{|\vec{\xi}|}\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{2}}
for the normalized directional derivative (with respect to
{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}
). For
Q^{1/2}/2\leq|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|\leq Q^{1/2}
, we have the following for
a,b,j\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\quad q^{j}\frac{\partial^{j}}{\partial q^{j}}\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}}^{a}\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}}^{b}p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})

\displaystyle\ll_{N}\mathds{1}_{\frac{kq}{Q}<1}\big(kqQ^{1/2}\big)^{a}Q^{3b/2}(1+|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|kqQ^{1/2}+|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}|Q)^{-N}.

Proof.

If
\frac{kq}{Q}\geq 1
, the result follows from the fact that

h(y,z)=0
when
y\geq 1
and
|z|\leq y/2
(see
[
14
, Lemma 4]
) together with the support of
\omega
and
\omega_{0}
.

Next we consider
\frac{kq}{Q}<1
.
We make a change of variables
{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}=z_{1}\vec{\mathrm{r}}+z_{2}\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}
to write

\displaystyle p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{Q^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega_{0}\left(\frac{|{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}|}{Q^{3/2}}\right)h\left(\frac{kq}{Q},\frac{\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}}{Q^{2}}\right)e(-{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{x}}})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}

(3.14)

\displaystyle=\frac{c|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|^{2}}{Q^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega_{0}\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}||{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}|}{Q^{3/2}}\right)h\left(\frac{kq}{Q},\frac{z_{1}|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|^{2}}{Q^{2}}\right)e(-{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot(z_{1}\vec{\mathrm{r}}+z_{2}\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}))\,d{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}.

Since
|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|\asymp Q^{1/2}
and
z_{1}\vec{\mathrm{r}}+z_{2}\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}=|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|(z_{1}\vec{\mathrm{r}}/|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|+z_{2}\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}/|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\quad|q^{j}\frac{\partial^{j}}{\partial q^{j}}\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}}^{a}\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}}^{b}p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})|

\displaystyle\ll Q^{-2}\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}|<Q^{3/2}/|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}(Q^{1/2}z_{1})^{a}(Q^{1/2}z_{2})^{b}\left|q^{j}\frac{\partial^{j}}{\partial q^{j}}h\left(\frac{kq}{Q},\frac{z_{1}|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|^{2}}{Q^{2}}\right)\right|dz_{1}dz_{2}

\displaystyle\ll\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}|<1}(Q^{3/2}z_{1})^{a}(Q^{3/2}z_{2})^{b}\left|q^{j}\frac{\partial^{j}}{\partial q^{j}}h\left(\frac{kq}{Q},z_{1}\right)\right|dz_{1}dz_{2}

\displaystyle\ll Q^{3b/2}\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}|<1}(Q^{3/2}z_{1})^{a}\left|q^{j}\frac{\partial^{j}}{\partial q^{j}}h\left(\frac{kq}{Q},z_{1}\right)\right|dz_{1}

\displaystyle\ll\left(kqQ^{1/2}\right)^{a}Q^{3b/2},

where in the last inequality we invoked
[
14
, Lemma 5]
, which gives for each
R,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{m}}{\partial y^{m}}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial z^{n}}h(y,z)

\displaystyle\ll_{n,m,R}y^{-1-m-n}\Big(y^{R}\mathds{1}_{n=0}+\min\{1,y^{R}|z|^{-R}\}\Big)

(3.15)

\displaystyle\ll y^{-1-m-n}\min\{1,y^{R}|z|^{-R}\},\quad\quad\text{if }|z|\leq 1,y<1.

It is therefore enough to check the decay properties of
p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}
, that is, for any
A\gg 1
such that either
kqQ^{1/2}|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\gg A
or
Q|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}|\gg A
we have

|q^{j}\frac{\partial^{j}}{\partial q^{j}}\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}}^{a}\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}}^{b}p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})|\ll_{N}\left(kqQ^{1/2}\right)^{a}Q^{3b/2}A^{-N}.

The condition
kqQ^{1/2}|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\gg A
holds if and only if
\max\{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}|,|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}|\}\gg\frac{A}{kq}
. Since
\frac{1}{kq}\geq Q^{-1}
, it is enough to consider the two cases

|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}|\gg\frac{A}{kq}\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{or}\,\,\,\,\,\,|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}|\gg A/Q.

First suppose that
|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}|\gg\frac{A}{kq}
.
After rewriting
p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}
in (
3.14
) and applying integration by parts in the
z_{1}
variable we obtain

\displaystyle\quad\frac{c|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|^{2}}{Q^{3}}q^{j}\frac{\partial^{j}}{\partial q^{j}}\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}}^{a}\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}}^{b}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega_{0}\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}||{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}|}{Q^{3/2}}\right)h\left(\frac{kq}{Q},\frac{z_{1}|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|^{2}}{Q^{2}}\right)e(-{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot(z_{1}\vec{\mathrm{r}}+z_{2}\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}))\,d{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{c|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|^{2}}{Q^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{e(-{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot(z_{1}\vec{\mathrm{r}}+z_{2}\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}))}{({\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}})^{N}}\frac{\partial^{N}}{\partial z_{1}^{N}}\Big(z_{1}^{a}z_{2}^{b}|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|^{a+b}\omega_{0}\big(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}||{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}|}{Q^{3/2}}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times q^{j}\frac{\partial^{j}}{\partial q^{j}}h\left(\frac{kq}{Q},\frac{z_{1}|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|^{2}}{Q^{2}}\right)\Big)\,d{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}

\displaystyle\ll Q^{(a+b)/2-2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\frac{kq}{A}\right)^{N}\Big|\frac{\partial^{N}}{\partial z_{1}^{N}}\big[z_{1}^{a}z_{2}^{b}\omega_{0}\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}||{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}|}{Q^{3/2}}\right)q^{j}\frac{\partial^{j}}{\partial q^{j}}h\left(\frac{kq}{Q},\frac{z_{1}|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|^{2}}{Q^{2}}\right)\big]\Big|\,d{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}.

We invoke (
3.15
) again to find that above is

\displaystyle\ll_{N,R}Q^{(a+3b)/2-2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}\geq 0\\
n_{1}\leq a\\
N=n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}\end{subarray}}\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}|\ll Q}\Big(\frac{kq}{A}\Big)^{N}z_{1}^{a-n_{1}}Q^{-n_{2}}\left(kq\right)^{-n_{3}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\big(\frac{Q}{kq}\big)\min\Big\{1,\frac{kq}{z_{1}}\Big\}^{R}\,d{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}

\displaystyle\ll_{N,R}Q^{(a+3b)/2}A^{-N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq n_{1}\leq a\\
0\leq n_{2}\leq N\end{subarray}}\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}|\ll 1}(kq)^{n_{1}+n_{2}}z_{1}^{a-n_{1}}Q^{a-n_{1}-n_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\big(\frac{Q}{kq}\big)\min\Big\{1,\frac{kq}{Qz_{1}}\Big\}^{R}d{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}

\displaystyle\ll_{N,R}\left(kqQ^{1/2}\right)^{a}Q^{3b/2}A^{-N}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega_{0}(|{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}|)\big(\frac{Q}{kq}\big)\min\Big\{1,\frac{kq}{Qz_{1}}\Big\}^{R}\,d{\vec{\mathrm{z}}},

where in the last line we use the fact that the term with
n_{1}=n_{2}=0
dominates the sum,

\frac{kq}{Q}<1
and that
R
can be arbitrarily large. The result follows in the case
|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}|\gg\frac{A}{kq}
, since the integral over
{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}
is
O(1)
.

The case
|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}|\gg A/Q
is similar. Integration by parts in
z_{2}
gives

\displaystyle\pm\frac{c|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|^{2}}{Q^{3}}q^{j}\frac{\partial^{j}}{\partial q^{j}}\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}}^{a}\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}}^{b}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega_{0}\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}||{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}|}{Q^{3/2}}\right)h\left(\frac{kq}{Q},\frac{z_{1}|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|^{2}}{Q^{2}}\right)e(-{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot(z_{1}\vec{\mathrm{r}}+z_{2}\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}))\,d{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}

\displaystyle=\pm Q^{(a+b)/2-2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{e(-{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot(z_{1}\vec{\mathrm{r}}+z_{2}\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}))}{({\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp})^{N}}\frac{\partial^{N}}{\partial z_{2}^{N}}\Big(z_{1}^{a}z_{2}^{b}\omega_{0}\big(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}||{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}|}{Q^{3/2}}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times q^{j}\frac{\partial^{j}}{\partial q^{j}}h\left(\frac{kq}{Q},\frac{z_{1}|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|^{2}}{Q^{2}}\right)\Big)\,d{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}

\displaystyle\ll_{N,R}Q^{(a+b)/2-2}A^{-N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq n_{1}\leq b,n_{2}\geq 0\\
N=n_{1}+n_{2}\end{subarray}}\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}|\ll Q}Q^{N}z_{1}^{a}z_{2}^{b-n_{1}}Q^{-n_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\big(\frac{Q}{kq}\big)\min\Big\{1,\frac{kq}{z_{1}}\Big\}^{R}d{\vec{\mathrm{z}}},

and we can complete the argument in the same way as before.
∎

To further understand the function
p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}
, we use Lemma
2.1
to obtain an asymptotic formula for
p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})
for certain ranges of
{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\vec{\mathrm{r}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},k,q\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta>0
. Suppose
|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|\asymp Q^{1/2}
,
kq\leq Q
, and
kq(Q|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}|+1)\leq Q^{1-\delta}
, then we have

\displaystyle p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})=\frac{cQ^{1/2}}{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}\widehat{\omega}_{0}\Big(\frac{Q^{3/2}}{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}\Big)+O_{N,\delta}(Q^{-N}).

Proof.

We have from (
3.14
) that

\displaystyle p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})=\frac{c|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|^{2}}{Q^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\omega_{0}\Big(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}||{\vec{\mathrm{z}}}|}{Q^{3/2}}\Big)h\Big(\frac{kq}{Q},\frac{z_{1}|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|^{2}}{Q^{2}}\Big)e(-{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot(z_{1}\vec{\mathrm{r}}+z_{2}\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}))dz_{1}dz_{2}.

The integral over
z_{1}
can be estimated using Lemma
2.1
with

\displaystyle y=\frac{kq}{Q},\quad z=z_{1},\quad A=Q^{-1}(Q|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}|+1),\quad\quad\quad

\displaystyle B=\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|^{2}}{Q^{2}},\quad f(z_{1})=\omega_{0}\Big(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|(z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2})^{1/2}}{Q^{3/2}}\Big)e(-z_{1}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}),

since the hypothesis in Lemma
2.1
is guaranteed because we chose
\omega_{0}
in such a way that
\omega_{0}(|x|^{1/2})
is smooth, and because of our assumption in the present lemma that

\frac{kq}{Q}\leq Q^{-\delta}(Q|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}|+1)^{-1}\asymp Q^{-\delta}B/A.

Then the result follows after integrating over
z_{2}
.
∎

4.
Two-dimensional delta symbol: Theorem
1.2

4.1.
The lattice
\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)

To prove Theorem
1.2
, we show various properties of the lattice
\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)
defined in (
1.4
), using the geometry of numbers. We first give some more detailed description of the lattice
\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)
.

4.1.1.
Description of
\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)

Let
q\in\mathbb{N},\vec{\mathrm{a}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with
\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)=1
. Recall the lattice
\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)
defined in (
1.4
).
It is easy to see that

\mathrm{covol}(\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q))=q,

since the lattice
\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)
is of index
q
in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
We begin by investigating relations among
\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)
for different
\vec{\mathrm{a}}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Given
q\in\mathbb{N},\vec{\mathrm{a}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with
\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)=1
, we have

(1)

\{\vec{\mathrm{b}}\bmod q:\vec{\mathrm{b}}\in\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q),\,\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{b}},q)=1\}=\{k\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod q:\gcd(k,q)=1\}
;

(2)

If
\vec{\mathrm{b}}\in\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)
with
\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{b}},q)=1
then

\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{b}},q)=\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)
;

(3)

\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)=\{\vec{\mathrm{r}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:q\mid\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\perp}\}.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{a}})=1
since otherwise we can replace
\vec{\mathrm{a}}
by
\vec{\mathrm{a}}/\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{a}})
since
\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{a}})
and
q
are co-prime.
Write
\mathbb{Z}^{2}=\mathbb{Z}\vec{\mathrm{a}}+\mathbb{Z}\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}
. Then we have
\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)=\mathbb{Z}\vec{\mathrm{a}}+\mathbb{Z}q\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}
.

If
\gcd(k,q)=1
then
\gcd(k\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)=1
and it follows that

\{k\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod q:\gcd(k,q)=1\}\subseteq\{\vec{\mathrm{b}}\bmod q:\vec{\mathrm{b}}\in\mathbb{Z}\vec{\mathrm{a}}+\mathbb{Z}q\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime},\,\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{b}},q)=1\}.

Conversely if
\vec{\mathrm{b}}\in\mathbb{Z}\vec{\mathrm{a}}+\mathbb{Z}q\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}
then
\vec{\mathrm{b}}\equiv k\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod q
for some
k
, and if
\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{b}},q)=1
then
\gcd(k,q)=1
. This proves the first equality, and the second equality follows since for
\gcd(k,q)=1
,

\{r\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod q:r\in\mathbb{Z}\}=\{rk\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod q:r\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

For the third equality, we observe that
\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\perp}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}=-\det(\vec{\mathrm{a}}|\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime})=\pm 1
and therefore

\displaystyle\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)=\mathbb{Z}\vec{\mathrm{a}}+\mathbb{Z}q\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\{\vec{\mathrm{r}}\in\mathbb{Z}\vec{\mathrm{a}}+\mathbb{Z}\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}:q\mid\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\perp}\}.

∎

4.1.2.
Geometry of numbers

We state a standard result from the geometry of numbers, which can be seen as a special case of Lemma 4.1 of Maynard
[
23
]
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\Lambda
be a lattice in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Let
M
be an
m\times 2
real matrix with full rank. Further let

{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{1}\in\Lambda
with
|M{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{1}|=\mu_{M}(\Lambda)=\min\{|M{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}|:{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}\in\Lambda,{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}\neq\vec{0}\}
. Then there is an
{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{2}\in\Lambda
satisfying the following

\displaystyle\ \Lambda

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Z}{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{1}+\mathbb{Z}{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{2},

\displaystyle|r_{1}M{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{1}+r_{2}M{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{2}|

\displaystyle\asymp_{m}|r_{1}||M{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{1}|+|r_{2}||M{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{2}|,\quad(\vec{\mathrm{r}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}),

\displaystyle|M{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{1}||M{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{2}|

\displaystyle\asymp_{m}\mathrm{covol}(\Lambda)\meas\{M[0,1]^{2}\}.

The key to bounding the contributions from the
p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}
terms is to apply Lemma
4.2
with
\Lambda=\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)
and a
3\times 2
matrix
M
defined as below:

Definition 4.3
.

Let
M=M({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})
be the
3\times 2
matrix such that for any
\vec{\mathrm{r}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}

(4.1)

M\vec{\mathrm{r}}=\left(\frac{r_{1}}{Q^{1/2}},\frac{r_{2}}{Q^{1/2}},Q{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}\right)^{T}.

Lemma 4.4
.

For
M
in defined in (
4.1
), we have

\meas\big\{M[0,1]^{2}\big\}\asymp Q^{1/2}|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|+Q^{-1}.

Proof.

Let
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
denote the standard basis for
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The measure of the parallelogram
M[0,1]^{2}
is equal to the norm of the vector

\displaystyle(Q^{-1/2}e_{1}+Qw_{2}e_{3})\wedge(Q^{-1/2}e_{2}-Qw_{1}e_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q^{-1}\,e_{1}\wedge e_{2}-Q^{1/2}w_{1}\,e_{1}\wedge e_{3}+Q^{1/2}w_{2}\,e_{3}\wedge e_{2},

which is

\sqrt{Q^{-2}+Q(w_{1}^{2}+w_{2}^{2})}\asymp Q^{-1}+Q^{1/2}|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|.

∎

4.2.
The function
p_{\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}
.

We are now ready to state the formula for
p_{\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}
and its properties.

4.2.1.
Definition of
p_{\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}

After replacing the sum over
\vec{\mathrm{r}}
in (
3.7
) by a sum over the lattice
\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)
using Lemma
4.1
, we can define
p_{\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}
as

(4.2)

\displaystyle p_{\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})

\displaystyle:=p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\mathrm{r}}\in\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)\\
k=\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}})/\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}},q)\end{subarray}}\omega\bigg(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\bigg)p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}}),

which proves (
1.5
).

4.2.2.
Asymptotic of
p_{\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}

To estimate
p_{\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}
, we first consider the sum over
\vec{\mathrm{r}}
in (
4.2
).

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\delta>0
and
M=M({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})
be as in (
4.1
) and let
\mu_{M}=\mu_{M}(\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q))
the norm of the smallest non-zero vector in
M(\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q))
. If

(4.3)

\mu_{M}\geq Q^{\delta}(qQ^{-1}+|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|qQ^{1/2}),

then

\begin{split}&\sum_{{\vec{\mathrm{r}}\in\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}}\omega\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\right)p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})+O_{N,\delta}(Q^{-N})\\
=&\frac{cQ^{1/2}}{q}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}\omega\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\right)\widehat{\omega}_{0}\Big(\frac{Q^{3/2}}{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}\Big)\,d\vec{\mathrm{r}}\end{split}

for any
N>0
.

Proof.

Throughout, we write
\vec{\mathrm{r}}=dk{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
, where
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is a primitive integer vector,
d=\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}},q)
and
\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}})=kd
with
\gcd(q/d,k)=1
.
First we prove that

(4.4)

\begin{split}&\sum_{{\vec{\mathrm{r}}\in\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}}\omega\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\right)p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})+O_{N,\delta}(Q^{-N})\\
=&\sum_{{\vec{\mathrm{r}}\in\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}}\frac{cQ^{1/2}}{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}\omega\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\right)\widehat{\omega}_{0}\Big(\frac{Q^{3/2}}{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}\Big).\end{split}

By Lemma
3.5
, the terms on each side with
k\leq\frac{Q^{-\delta/2}}{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|qQ^{1/2}+qQ^{-1}}
agree up to negligible error. We show that on each side of (
4.4
), there is only a negligible contribution from values of
\vec{\mathrm{r}}
with

(4.5)

k\geq\frac{Q^{-\delta/2}}{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|qQ^{1/2}+qQ^{-1}}.

Suppose that
\vec{\mathrm{r}}
satisfies
(
4.5
). It is easy to see that
\frac{\vec{\mathrm{r}}}{k}=d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\in{\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}
, and therefore

\left\lvert M(d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})\right\rvert\geq\mu_{M}.

This yields the penultimate inequality in

\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{Q^{1/2}}+Q|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}|\asymp|M\vec{\mathrm{r}}|\geq\mu_{M}k\geq{Q^{\delta/2}},

where the first equivalence holds by definition of
M
and the last inequality holds by
(
4.5
) and the assumption on
\mu_{M}
in the lemma.
Now using Lemma
3.4
, one can check that for any such
\vec{\mathrm{r}}
, we have

\displaystyle\omega\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\right)p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})

\displaystyle\ll_{N,\delta}Q^{-N},

\displaystyle\ \omega\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\right)\widehat{\omega}_{0}\Big(\frac{Q^{3/2}}{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}\Big)

\displaystyle\ll_{N,\delta}Q^{-N},

as required, verifying (
4.4
).

We next use the Poisson summation formula to prove that the sum on the right hand side of (
4.4
) may be replaced by an integral up to an admissible error. Let
X=({\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{1}|{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{2})
be the matrix with columns given by the vectors
{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{i}
from Lemma
4.2
for the lattice
\Lambda=\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)
and
M
. By (
4.4
) it suffices to prove

\displaystyle\sum_{{\vec{\mathrm{r}}\in\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}}\frac{cQ^{1/2}}{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}\omega\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\right)\widehat{\omega}_{0}\Big(\frac{Q^{3/2}}{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{cQ^{1/2}}{q}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}\omega\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\right)\widehat{\omega}_{0}\Big(\frac{Q^{3/2}}{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}\Big)\,d\vec{\mathrm{r}}+O_{N,\delta}(Q^{-N}).

We can re-write the left-hand side above as

\displaystyle\sum_{{\vec{\mathrm{y}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}W(MX{\vec{\mathrm{y}}}),\text{ where }W((x,y,z)^{T})=\frac{c}{|({}^{x}_{y})|}\omega\big(\lvert({}^{x}_{y})\rvert\big)\widehat{\omega}_{0}\big(|({}^{x}_{y})|^{-1}z\big).

By Lemma
4.2
and the fact that
\mathrm{covol}(\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q))=q
, we have

\displaystyle|M{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{1}||{\vec{\mathrm{y}}}|\ll|M{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{1}||y_{1}|+|M{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{2}||y_{2}|\asymp|MX{\vec{\mathrm{y}}}|

\displaystyle\ll|M{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{2}||{\vec{\mathrm{y}}}|\ll\frac{q\meas\{M[0,1]^{2}\}}{|M{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{1}|}|{\vec{\mathrm{y}}}|.

From the assumption
\mu_{M}=|M{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}_{1}|\geq Q^{\delta}(qQ^{1/2}|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|+qQ^{-1})
together with Lemma
4.4
, we obtain

|MX{\vec{\mathrm{y}}}|\ll Q^{-\delta}|{\vec{\mathrm{y}}}|.

Using this bound, a routine application of Poisson summation and integration by parts (or “non-stationary phase”) shows that

\sum_{{\vec{\mathrm{y}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}W(MX{\vec{\mathrm{y}}})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}W(MX{\vec{\mathrm{y}}})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{y}}}+O_{N,\delta}(Q^{-N}),

which concludes the result.
∎

Next we apply Lemma
4.5
to prove that under the assumption (
4.3
), the function
p_{\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}
is equal to
1
, up to a very small error, which implies (
1.6
).

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
M=M({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})
and
\mu_{M}=\mu_{M}(\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q))
be as in Lemma
4.5
. Let
1\leq q\leq Q
and
\delta>0
. If

\mu_{M}\geq Q^{\delta}(qQ^{-1}+|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|qQ^{1/2}),

then

p_{\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})=1+O_{N,\delta}(Q^{-N}),

for any
N>0
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.5
, and making a change of variables using
\vec{\mathrm{r}}=z^{\prime}{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}_{\theta},\ {\vec{\mathrm{v}}}_{\theta}=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta),\ z^{\prime}=|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|
, we obtain

\displaystyle\quad p_{\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})-p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{cQ^{1/2}}{q}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}\omega\left(\frac{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{Q^{1/2}}\right)\widehat{\omega}_{0}\Big(\frac{Q^{3/2}}{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}\Big)\,d\vec{\mathrm{r}}+O_{N,\delta}(Q^{-N})

\displaystyle=\frac{cQ^{1/2}}{q}\int_{0}^{\infty}\omega\Big(\frac{z^{\prime}}{Q^{1/2}}\Big)dz^{\prime}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\widehat{\omega}_{0}\Big(Q^{3/2}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}_{\theta}^{\perp}\Big)d\theta+O_{N,\delta}(Q^{-N})

\displaystyle=\frac{cQ}{q}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\omega_{0}(z)e\Big(-zQ^{3/2}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}_{\theta}^{\perp}\Big)dzd\theta+O_{N,\delta}(Q^{-N}).

By Lemmas
4.2
and
4.4
, we have

(4.6)

\displaystyle\mu_{M}\ll(qQ^{-1}+|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|qQ^{1/2})^{1/2}.

Therefore, by the assumption on
\mu_{M}
we have

q\ll Q^{1-\delta}
, and therefore upon substituting
z=Q^{3/2}z
we get

\displaystyle\quad p_{\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})-p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})+O_{N,\delta}(Q^{-N})

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{Q^{3/2}}\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\omega\left(\frac{qj}{Q}\right)\right)\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\omega}_{0}\left(\frac{z}{Q^{3/2}}\right)e\left(-zQ^{3/2}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}_{\theta}^{\perp}\right)dzd\theta.

Note that by making a change of variable
{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}\mapsto{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}^{\perp}
in
p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})
defined in (
3.8
), and further changing to polar coordinates, we can write

(4.7)

\begin{split}&p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})=-\frac{2c}{Q}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega_{0}\left(\frac{|{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}|}{Q^{3/2}}\right)\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{q^{2}j^{2}}\omega\left(\frac{|{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}|}{jqQ^{1/2}}\right)e(-{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}^{\perp})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}\\
&=-\frac{2c}{Q}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}z\omega_{0}\Big(\frac{z}{Q^{3/2}}\Big)\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{q^{2}j^{2}}\omega\left(\frac{z}{jqQ^{1/2}}\right)e\Big(-z{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}_{\theta}^{\perp}\Big)\,dzd\theta.\end{split}

It is straight-forward to see, by replacing
\theta
with
\theta+\pi
, that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}z\omega_{0}\Big(\frac{z}{Q^{3/2}}\Big)\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{q^{2}j^{2}}\omega\left(\frac{z}{jqQ^{1/2}}\right)e\Big(-z{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}_{\theta}^{\perp}\Big)\,dzd\theta

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}z\omega_{0}\Big(\frac{z}{Q^{3/2}}\Big)\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{q^{2}j^{2}}\omega\left(\frac{z}{jqQ^{1/2}}\right)e\Big(z{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}_{\theta}^{\perp}\Big)\,dzd\theta.

As
\omega_{0}
is even, we may therefore re-write (
4.7
) as

\displaystyle p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})=-\frac{c}{Q}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|z|\omega_{0}\Big(\frac{z}{Q^{3/2}}\Big)\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{q^{2}j^{2}}\omega\left(\frac{|z|}{jqQ^{1/2}}\right)e\big(-z{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}_{\theta}^{\perp}\big)\,dzd\theta.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\quad\quad p_{\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})+O_{N,\delta}(Q^{-N})

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{Q^{\frac{3}{2}}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\omega\big(\frac{qj}{Q}\big)-\frac{Q^{\frac{1}{2}}|z|}{j^{2}q^{2}}\omega\big(\frac{|z|}{jqQ^{1/2}}\big)\Big)\omega_{0}\big(\frac{z}{Q^{3/2}}\big)e(-z{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}_{\theta}^{\perp})dzd\theta,

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{Q^{\frac{3}{2}}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\omega_{0}\big(\frac{z}{Q^{3/2}}\big)h_{2}\big(\frac{q}{Q},\frac{z}{Q^{3/2}}\big)e\big(-z{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}_{\theta}^{\perp}\big)dzd\theta,

where

\displaystyle h_{2}(y,z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{yj}\left({yj}\omega\left(yj\right)-\frac{|z|}{yj}\omega\left(\frac{|z|}{yj}\right)\right).

Using the assumption in the lemma and (
4.6
), we have

qQ^{-1}+|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|qQ^{1/2}\ll Q^{-2\delta}
, so that we can apply Lemma
2.1
to the
z
-integral with

\displaystyle\omega_{1}(x)={x\omega(x)},\quad y=\frac{q}{Q},\quad A=Q^{-3/2}+|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|,\quad

\displaystyle B=Q^{-3/2},\quad f(z)=\omega_{0}\Big(\frac{z}{Q^{3/2}}\Big)e\Big(-z{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}_{\theta}^{\perp}\Big),

and conclude that

\begin{split}p_{\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})&=c\cdot\omega_{0}(0)e(0)\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}x\omega(x)\,dx\,d\theta+O_{N,\delta}(Q^{-N})\\
&=1+O_{N,\delta}(Q^{-N}),\end{split}

which proves the lemma.
∎

4.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.2

Equation (
1.5
) follows from (
4.2
). Applying Lemma
4.6
and noting that
\mu_{M}\geq Q^{-1/2}
, we obtain (
1.6
). The bound
(
1.7
) follows from Lemma
3.3
and (
1.8
) follows from Lemma
3.4
. (
1.9
) follows from splitting
p_{\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}}.q)}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})
into two sums using (
4.2
), switching the order of summation, separating the two sums and in the second sum, upon further writing
\vec{\mathrm{r}}=kd{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
with
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
primitive,
\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}},q)=d
, so that
\gcd(q/d,k)=1
.

5.
Rational points: preparing for Theorem
1.1

All the notation introduced in the next three subsections will be used throughout the remainder of this paper.
In particular the reader will frequently encounter a distinction between four kinds of primes: bad primes, good primes for bad
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
, type I primes, and type II primes. These terms are defined here, in Section
5.3
. Other conventions and definitions are found in sections
2
and
11.1
.

5.1.
The major and minor arcs and a version of Theorem
1.2

Let
\delta>0
be fixed but sufficiently small, to be chosen later.
In view of the properties (
1.6
) and (
1.8
), we define the major arcs as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{q}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{M}_{q}(\delta)=\Big\{{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|<q^{-1}Q^{-1-\delta}\Big\}

\displaystyle\text{ if }1\leq q\leq Q^{1/2-\delta},

and the minor arcs as

(5.1)

\displaystyle\quad\mathfrak{m}_{q}=\mathfrak{m}_{q}(\delta)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\big\{{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:q^{-1}Q^{-1-\delta}\leq|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|<q^{-1}Q^{-1/2+\delta}\big\}&\text{ if }1\leq q\leq Q^{1/2-\delta},\\
\big\{{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|<q^{-1}Q^{-1/2+\delta}\big\}&\text{ if }Q^{1/2-\delta}<q\leq Q.\end{cases}

Most authors do not take major and minor arcs in the
\delta
-method, and indeed they are a little different to the classical case.
Note that for us the arcs
\mathfrak{M}_{q},\mathfrak{m}_{q}
are all centred at
\vec{0}
. The traditional (shifted) major and minor arcs would be
\vec{\mathrm{a}}/q+\mathfrak{M}_{q}
and
\vec{\mathrm{a}}/q+\mathfrak{m}_{q}
for
(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)=1
. Contrary to the classical circle method, here the union of the shifted major arcs
\mathfrak{M}=\bigcup_{q=1}^{Q^{1/2-\delta}}\bigcup_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}}^{*}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}/q+{\mathfrak{M}}_{q})
is not disjoint from the union of the shifted minor arcs
\mathfrak{m}=\bigcup_{q=1}^{Q}\bigcup_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}}^{*}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}/q+{\mathfrak{m}}_{q})
. This is acceptable because, on each arc, we integrate with respect to a different kernel function
p_{\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}
.

We begin by noting that using (
1.6
), on major arcs the function
p_{\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}
is equal to
1
with a very small error. On the minor arcs, it would be preferable for us to not work with the kernel function
p_{\Lambda(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)}
directly. We will instead apply the following proposition which is an easy corollary of Theorem
1.2
. In particular, it follows from a combination of (
1.6
) and (
1.9
). This would allow us to work with known exponential sums and also draw parallels with
[
37
, Theorem 1.1]
and further allow us to re-use some of the bounds obtained in
[
37
]
over minor arcs. More precisely, the sum
E_{2}
appearing in Proposition
5.1
will lead to the term
N_{2}(P,\delta)
appearing in (
5.5
), which corresponds closely with
[
37
, Lemma 5.1]
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
\vec{\mathrm{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and let
Q\geq 1
be a large parameter. Then for any
\delta,N>0
with
\delta
sufficiently small, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}=M+E_{1}+E_{2}+O_{N,\delta}(Q^{-\delta N}),

where

\begin{split}M&=\sum_{1\leq q\leq Q^{1/2-\delta}}\,\,\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod q}\int_{{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathfrak{M}_{q}}e((\vec{\mathrm{a}}/q+{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\\
E_{1}&=\sum_{1\leq q\leq Q}\,\,\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod q}\int_{{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathfrak{m}_{q}}p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})e((\vec{\mathrm{a}}/q+{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\\
E_{2}&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d,k\in\mathbb{N}\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\mathrm{primitive}\\
\vec{\mathrm{r}}=dk{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\end{subarray}}\omega\big(\frac{\vec{\mathrm{r}}}{Q^{1/2}}\big)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq q\leq Q/k\\
d\mid q\\
\gcd(q/d,k)=1\end{subarray}}\,\,\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod q\\
q\mid d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\perp}\end{subarray}}\\
&\quad\quad\times\int_{{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathfrak{m}_{q}}p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})e((\vec{\mathrm{a}}/q+{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\end{split}

with
\omega
as in Theorem
1.2
and the functions
p_{1,q}
and
p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}
as defined as in Lemma
3.2
, satisfying decay properties (
1.7
) and (
1.8
).

5.2.
Reduction of Theorem
1.1
to the sums
N_{i}

For the rest of this paper we adopt the following conventions. We
write
{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}=(F_{1},F_{2})
for a pair of forms of degree
d\geq 2
. We let
P\geq 1
be a large parameter and we let
w\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{s})
be a smooth function with a compact support. We work with the counting function

N(P):=N_{{\vec{\mathrm{F}}},w}(P):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}\\
{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x})=\vec{0}\end{subarray}}w(\mathbf{x}/P)=\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}}w(\mathbf{x}/P)\delta_{{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x})}.

We choose
Q
such that
Q^{1+1/2}=P^{d}
. Starting with Lemma
5.3
we only consider the case
d=2
, and so we set
Q=P^{4/3}
from then on.

Lemma 5.2
.

With the notation above, we have, for any sufficiently small
\delta>0
, that

(5.2)

N(P)=N_{0}(P,\delta)+N_{1}(P,\delta)+N_{2}(P,\delta)+O_{N,\delta}(Q^{-N\delta}),

where

(5.3)

\displaystyle N_{0}(P,\delta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq q<Q^{1/2-\delta}}q^{-s}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}\end{subarray}}D_{q}(\mathbf{u})\int_{{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathfrak{M}_{q}(\delta)}I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}},

(5.4)

\displaystyle N_{1}(P,\delta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq q<Q}q^{-s}\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}}D_{q}(\mathbf{u})\int_{\mathfrak{m}_{q}(\delta)}p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}},

(5.5)

\displaystyle N_{2}(P,\delta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d,k\in\mathbb{N}\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\mathrm{primitive}\\
\vec{\mathrm{r}}=dk{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\end{subarray}}\omega\big(\frac{\vec{\mathrm{r}}}{Q^{1/2}}\big)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq q\leq Q/k\\
d\mid q\\
\gcd(q/d,k)=1\end{subarray}}q^{-s}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}}S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})\int_{{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathfrak{m}_{q}(\delta)}p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}},

where the exponential sums
D_{q},S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}
and the exponential integral
I_{q}
are defined as

(5.6)

\displaystyle D_{q}(\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod q}\sum_{\mathbf{b}\bmod q}e_{q}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{b})+\mathbf{b}\cdot\mathbf{u}),

(5.7)

\displaystyle S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod q\\
q\mid d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\perp}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\mathbf{b}\bmod q}e_{q}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{b})+\mathbf{b}\cdot\mathbf{u}),

(5.8)

\displaystyle I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{s}}w\left(\frac{\mathbf{x}}{P}\right)e({\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))e_{q}(-\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{x}.

Proof.

Applying Proposition
5.1
, we obtain (
5.2
)
where

\displaystyle\quad N_{0}(P,\delta)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{1\leq q<Q^{1/2-\delta}}\,\,{\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{q}}}\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}}e_{q}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\int_{{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathfrak{M}_{q}(\delta)}w(\mathbf{x}/P)e({\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}},

\displaystyle\quad N_{1}(P,\delta)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{1\leq q\leq Q}\underset{\hphantom{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{q}}}{\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{q}}}\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}}e_{q}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\int_{{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathfrak{m}_{q}(\delta)}p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})w(\mathbf{x}/P)e({\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}},

\displaystyle\quad N_{2}(P,\delta)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d,k\in\mathbb{N}\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\text{ primitive }\\
\vec{\mathrm{r}}=dk{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\end{subarray}}\omega\big(\frac{\vec{\mathrm{r}}}{Q^{1/2}}\big)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq q\leq Q/k\\
d\mid q\\
\gcd(q/d,k)=1\end{subarray}}\underset{\hphantom{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{q}}}{\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{q}\\
q\mid d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\perp}\end{subarray}}}\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}}e_{q}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\int_{{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathfrak{m}_{q}(\delta)}w(\mathbf{x}/P)p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})e({\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}.

The lemma follows by applying Poisson summation in the
\mathbf{x}
variable with modulus
q
.
∎

The process of proving the estimates for
N_{1}(P,\delta)
and
N_{2}(P,\delta)
from the minor arcs in Lemma
5.3

is similar to that in
[
37
]
. However, the current situation is more complicated due to the different weight functions
p_{1,q},p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}
appearing in
N_{1}(P,\delta),N_{2}(P,\delta)
in (
5.4
)-(
5.5
). Our bounds for the
p
-functions get increasingly worse as the sizes of
q
and
|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|
decrease. However, in the extreme case

q\asymp Q,\ |{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\asymp q^{-1}Q^{-1/2+\delta},

our bounds match those in
[
37
]
.

In the rest of the paper our principal task will be to
prove the following lemma by investigating the exponential sum
D_{q},S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}
and the exponential integral
I_{q}
. This lemma immediately proves Theorem
1.1
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be any constant. With the notation from Lemma
5.2
, for all sufficiently small
\delta
(depending on
s
and
\varepsilon
)
we have

(a)

N_{0}(P,\delta)=P^{s-4}\mathfrak{S}\mathfrak{J}+O\Big(P^{s-4-1/3+\varepsilon}\Big)
;

(b)

N_{1}(P,\delta)\ll P^{s-4-(s-8)/3+\varepsilon};

(c)

N_{2}(P,\delta)\ll\begin{cases}P^{s-4-(s-10)/3-1/6+\varepsilon}&\text{ for even $s$ and $s\geq 10$},\\
P^{s-4-(s-9)/3-1/15+\varepsilon}&\text{ for odd $s$ and $s\geq 9$ under GLH,}\\
P^{s-4-(s-11)/3-1/15+\varepsilon}&\text{ for odd $s$ and $s\geq 11$.}\end{cases}

Here all the implicit constants may depend on
s,w,F_{1},F_{2}
and
\varepsilon
.

The proof of parts (
a
) and (
b
) resembles the circle method with Kloosterman refinement and arcs of the usual “Dirichlet” size
[
14
,
16
]
, whereas part (
c
) has more in common with the nested delta-method
[
6
]
or the function field two-dimensional delta-method
[
37
, Lemma 5.1, section 6]
. The structure of the proof of Lemma
5.3
is summarized in Figure
2
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Combining (
5.2
) with Lemma
5.3
, we obtain the result by choosing
\delta
sufficiently small.
∎

5.3.
Geometry of pairs of quadratic forms, and four sorts of primes

To estimate the exponential sums and exponential integrals in Lemma
5.2
, we need some properties of the geometry of non-singular intersection of two quadrics. The reader may consult the summary of Browning–Munshi
[
6
, §2.2]
as well as Heath-Brown–Pierce
[
16
, Section 2]
. Suppose
{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}=(F_{1},F_{2})
is a pair of quadratic forms in
s
variables such that the projective variety
1
1

1

By a variety over
K
, we mean a
reduced
separated scheme of finite type over
\Spec K
. In fact, all varieties occurring here will be quasiprojective; thus the reader is free to interpret
variety
as an
open subset of a reduced, not necessarily irreducible, projective variety
.
defined by
F_{1}(\mathbf{x})=F_{2}(\mathbf{x})=0
is non-singular over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
. Let
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
denote integer matrices defining the quadratic forms
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
. This projective variety has a dual variety, which is an absolutely irreducible hypersurface of degree
4(s-2)
when
s\geq 4
. (See
[
6
, §2.2]
for more details; the proof implicitly requires the absolute irreducibility of the smooth complete intersection
F_{1}=F_{2}=0
, which holds for
s\geq 4
, and the result of Aznar referred to there is a composite of
[
3
, Theorems 2-3]
.)

In what follows we will therefore take the dual variety to be defined by
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0
, where
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}
is a homogeneous polynomial with integer coefficients of degree
4(s-2)
which is irreducible over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
.
Given a primitive integer vector
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
, let
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
be the quadratic form

F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}:=c_{1}F_{1}+c_{2}F_{2},

defined by the integer matrix

M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}:=c_{1}M_{1}+c_{2}M_{2}.

Let
F(x,y)=\det(xF_{1}+yF_{2})
and

D_{F}=2\text{Disc}(F)

where
\text{Disc}(F)
denotes the discriminant of
F(x,y)
as a binary form.

Over the splitting field
K
we have

\displaystyle\det(xF_{1}+yF_{2})=h^{-1}\prod_{i=1}^{s}(\lambda_{i}x+\mu_{i}y),

for some
h\in\mathbb{N},\lambda_{i},\mu_{i}\in\mathscr{O}_{K}
.

Let
S,T\in\operatorname{GL}(s,\mathbb{Z})
be invertible integral matrices appearing in a Smith normal form of the matrix
M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}=TDS
where
D
is a diagonal integer matrix with entries
\rho_{1}|\cdots|\rho_{s}
. For
\det(M_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}})\neq 0
, let
F^{*}_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
denote the dual form for the quadratic form
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
defined by

(5.9)

F^{*}_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathbf{u}):=\det(M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})\mathbf{u}^{T}M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{-1}\mathbf{u}.

For a fixed
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
, this is a quadratic polynomial in the variable
\mathbf{u}
. For a fixed
\mathbf{u}
, this is a polynomial of degree
s-1
in the projective variable
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
whose discriminant is given by the equation of the dual variety
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})
.

From
[
16
, Section 2]
, we know that
\operatorname{rank}M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\geq s-1
by the non-singular condition on
{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}
. A primitive integer pair
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is called bad if the matrix
M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is singular, otherwise it is called good. Note that there are at most
s
pairs of bad
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
. We divide the primes into the following categories:

(1)

bad primes:

p\mid D_{F}
,

(2)

good primes for bad
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
:

p\nmid D_{F}
,

(3)

good primes of Type I for good
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
:

p\nmid D_{F},p\nmid\det(M_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}})
,

(4)

good primes of Type II for good
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
:

p\nmid D_{F},p\mid\det(M_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}})
.

When
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is bad, the form
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is singular and in particular
\rho_{s}=0
. Similarly when
p
is a good prime of type II for good
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
, we have
p\mid\det(M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})
and thus
p\mid\rho_{s}
. Let
\mathbf{y}_{j}=S^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{j}
where
\{\mathbf{e}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{s}
denotes the standard basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{s}
and
S
is the invertible integer matrix appearing in the Smith normal form for
M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
. Note that
\{\mathbf{y}_{i}\}_{j=1}^{s}
also forms a basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{s}
. Let
Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
denote the restriction of
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
to the
s-1
dimensional subspace spanned by
\mathbf{y}_{1},...,\mathbf{y}_{s-1}
. Namely,

Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}(y_{1},...,y_{s-1}):=F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}(y_{1}\mathbf{y}_{1}+...+y_{s-1}\mathbf{y}_{s-1}).

Similarly,
Q^{*}_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
denotes the dual form to the quadratic form
Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
. Given
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{s}
, let
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
denote the
s-1
dimensional vector by deleting the
s
-th entry of
(S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u}
.
Since
\{\mathbf{y}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{s}
forms a basis for
\mathbb{Z}^{s}
, if
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}
, so is the vector
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
.

We remark that for bad
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
and good
p
, if we write
\Delta(Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})
for the determinant of
Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
, then we have
p\nmid\Delta(Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})
. Indeed if
p\mid\Delta(Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})
then the rank of
M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
over
\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
is
\leq s-2
, and so
F_{1}=F_{2}=0
is singular over
\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
and
D_{F}=0
over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
by
[
16
, Proposition 2.1]
, hence
p\mid D_{F}
.

For a non-zero vector
{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}
, define

(5.10)

\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}:=\min_{1\leq j\leq s}\{|(\lambda_{j},\mu_{j})\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|/|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\},

which measures the distance of the unit vector
{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}/|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|
from the lines
\lambda_{j}x+\mu_{j}y=0
.
Note
\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}=0
if and only if
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is bad.

Before going into the details, we summarize in Table
1
the conditions on the number of variables
s
required for our method. Although
s
must be a natural number of the indicated parity, the lower bound formally required for the last step in the argument can serve as a measure of the difficulty in making further improvements.

Limits of the argument.

6.
Bounds for quadratic exponential integrals

Recall the exponential integral defined in (
5.8
). After a change of variables, we see that

I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})=P^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{s}}w\left(\mathbf{x}\right)e(P^{2}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))e(-P\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{u}/q)d\mathbf{x}.

We have the following pointwise bound for
I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})
. This will be used to study
N_{0}(P,\delta)
and also to prove some sharper estimates later in this section.

Lemma 6.1
.

If we let

\delta>0
, then for
|\mathbf{u}|\geq\frac{q}{P}(1+P^{2}|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|)P^{\delta}
, we have

I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})\ll_{N}P^{s}(P|\mathbf{u}|/q)^{-N}\ll P^{s-N\delta}

for any
N>0
.
Moreover for any
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{s}
,

\displaystyle|I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})|

\displaystyle\ll P^{s}\prod_{j=1}^{s}(1+P^{2}|\lambda_{j}w_{1}+\mu_{j}w_{2}|)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\ll P^{s}(1+P^{2}|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|)^{-(s-1)/2}(1+P^{2}|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})^{-1/2},

where
\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}
is defined in (
5.10
).

Proof.

The lemma follows from
[
16
, Lemma 3.1, Lemma 2.3]
.
∎

We also need some estimates of averaged bounds for
I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})
integrated against
p_{1,q}
and
p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}
. The following bound will be used to bound
N_{1}(P,\delta)
.

Lemma 6.2
.

For any
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{s}
we have

\displaystyle\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\asymp W}|p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})||I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})|d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\ll\frac{Q}{q}W^{2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-s/2-1}(1+|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|qQ^{1/2})^{-N}

for any
N>0
.

Proof.

The lemma follows from
[
16
, Lemma 3.3]
together with (
1.7
) on the decay properties of
p_{1,q}
and the relation
P^{2}=Q^{3/2}
.
∎

Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\vec{\mathrm{r}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, we write for short

\displaystyle p_{2}(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})=:p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}}).

The rest of the section will be dedicated to bounds which will be useful towards bounding
N_{2}(P,\delta)
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\vec{\mathrm{r}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with
|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|\asymp Q^{1/2}
and
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}=\vec{\mathrm{r}}/\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}})
. Let
\mu_{S^{1}}
be the Lebesgue measure (arc-length measure) on the unit circle
S^{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
For any
W>0
, we have for
j=0,1

\displaystyle\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\asymp W}|q^{j}\frac{\partial^{j}}{\partial q^{j}}p_{2}(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})||I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})|d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}

\displaystyle\ll\mathds{1}_{\frac{kq}{Q}<1}WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+kqQ^{1/2}W)^{-N}(1+P^{2}W)^{-\frac{(s-1)}{2}}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2},

and, where
\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is as in (
5.10
),

\displaystyle W^{2}\int_{S^{1}}|W^{j}\frac{\partial^{j}}{\partial W^{j}}p_{2}(q,W{\vec{\mathrm{v}}})||I_{q}(W{\vec{\mathrm{v}}},\mathbf{u})|d\mu_{S^{1}}({\vec{\mathrm{v}}})

\displaystyle\ll\mathds{1}_{\frac{kq}{Q}<1}WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+kqQ^{1/2}W)^{-N}(1+P^{2}W)^{-\frac{(s-1)}{2}}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2},

the right hand side being the same in both of these two estimates.

Proof.

For
{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}=(w_{1},w_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we define its radial coordinates by
{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}=W{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}
with
W\geq 0
and
{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}\in S^{1}
.

We begin by considering the derivative
\frac{\partial}{\partial W}p_{2}(q,W{\vec{\mathrm{v}}})
for
{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}\in S^{1}
.
For fixed
{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}
, we have the identity
\frac{d}{dW}f(x(W),y(W))=\frac{dx}{dW}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}+\frac{dy}{dW}\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}
with
x(W)=\frac{\vec{\mathrm{r}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}}{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}
,
y(W)=\frac{\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}}{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}
and
f(x,y)=p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})
, which gives

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial W}p_{2}(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\vec{\mathrm{r}}}{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}\cdot\frac{dx(W)}{dW}\right)\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}}p_{2}(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})+\left(\frac{\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}}{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}\cdot\frac{dy(W)}{dW}\right)\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}}p_{2}(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}}{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}}p_{2}(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})+\frac{{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}}{|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|}\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}}p_{2}(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}),

upon writing
\partial_{\vec{\xi}}=\frac{\xi_{1}}{|\vec{\xi}|}\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{1}}+\frac{\xi_{2}}{|\vec{\xi}|}\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{2}}
.
This gives

W\frac{\partial}{\partial W}p_{2}(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})=O(W)\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}}p_{2}(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})+O\left(\frac{|W{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}|}{Q^{1/2}}\right)\partial_{\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}}p_{2}(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}).

By Lemma
3.4
, it follows that

\displaystyle W\frac{\partial}{\partial W}p_{2}(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})

\displaystyle\ll(kqQ^{1/2}W+{|W{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}|}Q)(1+kqQ^{1/2}W)^{-N}(1+|W{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}|Q)^{-N}

\displaystyle\ll(1+kqQ^{1/2}W+{|W{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}|}Q)(1+kqQ^{1/2}W)^{-N}(1+|W{\vec{\mathrm{v}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}|Q)^{-N}

for any
N>0
. Thus for any
N>0
,

(6.1)

W\frac{\partial}{\partial W}p_{2}(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})\ll(1+kqQ^{1/2}W)^{-N+1}(1+|W{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{r}}^{\perp}|Q)^{-N+1}.

Now let the differential operator
D
be defined either by
D=q\frac{\partial}{\partial q}
, or by
D=W\frac{\partial}{\partial W}
.
From Lemma
6.1
and from Lemma
3.4
for
D=q\frac{\partial}{\partial q}
and (
6.1
) for
D=W\frac{\partial}{\partial W}
, we obtain the following estimate for
j=0,1
,
kq<Q
and
\tau\in(1,2]
:

\displaystyle\int_{W\leq|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\leq\tau W}|D^{j}p_{2}(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})||I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})|d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}

\displaystyle\ll_{N}P^{s}\int_{W\leq|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\leq\tau W}(1+kqQ^{1/2}|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|)^{-N}(1+Q\gcd(\vec{\mathrm{r}})|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{\perp}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|)^{-N}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\prod_{j=1}^{s}(1+P^{2}|\lambda_{i}w_{1}+\mu_{i}w_{2}|)^{-1/2}d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}.

We change variables to write
{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}=x\frac{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}{|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|}+y\frac{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{\perp}}{|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|}
so that

\displaystyle\int_{W\leq|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\leq\tau W}|D^{j}p_{2}(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})||I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})|d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}

\displaystyle\ll_{N}P^{s}\int_{W\leq|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\leq\tau W}(1+kqQ^{1/2}x)^{-N}(1+Q^{3/2}y)^{-N}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\prod_{j=1}^{s}\Big(1+P^{2}|(\lambda_{j},\mu_{j})\cdot(x\frac{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}{|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|}+y\frac{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{\perp}}{|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|})|\Big)^{-1/2}dxdy,

which is

\displaystyle\ll_{N}P^{s}Q^{-3/2}\int_{W\leq x\leq\tau W}(1+kqQ^{1/2}x)^{-N}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\prod_{j=1}^{s}(1+P^{2}|(\lambda_{j},\mu_{j})\cdot(x{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}/|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|)|)^{-1/2}dx,

and using
[
16
, Lemma 2.3]
this is

\ll(\tau-1)WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+kqQ^{1/2}W)^{-N}(1+P^{2}W)^{-\frac{(s-1)}{2}}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}.

Taking

D=q\frac{\partial}{\partial q}
,
this concludes the first part of the lemma. Alternatively, dividing both sides by
(\tau-1)
, letting
\tau
go to
1
, and taking
D=W\frac{\partial}{\partial W}
, we obtain the second part of the lemma.
∎

When
|\mathbf{u}|\leq\frac{q}{P}(1+P^{2}W)P^{\varepsilon},
we will also need a bound on average value of the derivative
\frac{\partial}{\partial q}I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})
integrated against
p_{2}(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})
when we consider a double Kloosterman refinement to make use of cancellations from the
q
-sum.

Lemma 6.4
.

Under the same assumptions as in Lemma
6.3
, we have the following bound for any
N\geq 0
and any interval
I\subseteq[1,2]

\displaystyle\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|/W\in I}p_{2}(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})\frac{\partial}{\partial q}I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}

\displaystyle\ll\mathds{1}_{\frac{kq}{Q}<1}q^{-1}WQ^{-\frac{3}{2}}P^{s}(1+kqQ^{1/2}W)^{-N}(1+P^{2}W)^{-\frac{(s-1)}{2}}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Proof.

We note that

\frac{\partial}{\partial q}I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})=q^{-1}P^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{s}}\frac{2\pi iP\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{u}}{q}w\left(\mathbf{x}\right)e(P^{2}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))e(-P\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{u}/q)d\mathbf{x}.

Treating the factor
\frac{P\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{u}}{q}
trivially will result in an extra factor of
1+P^{2}|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|
in case
\frac{P|\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{x}|}{q}\ll P^{\varepsilon}(1+P^{2}|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|)
, as Lemma
6.1
suffices otherwise.
The goal is to remove the factor
P\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{u}/q
and we achieve this by integration by parts.
We first write

\displaystyle q\frac{\partial}{\partial q}I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=-P^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{s}}w\left(\mathbf{x}\right)e(P^{2}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla e(-P\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{u}/q)\,d\mathbf{x}\,

\displaystyle=P^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{s}}\text{div}\left(w\left(\mathbf{x}\right)e(P^{2}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))\mathbf{x}\right)e(-P\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{u}/q)\,d\mathbf{x},

where we applied the divergence theorem in the second equality. Now we use the fact that
{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}
is homogeneous, so that
\sum_{j=1}^{s}x_{j}w(\mathbf{x})\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}e(P^{2}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))=4\pi iP^{2}w(\mathbf{x}){\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}).
It follows that

\displaystyle q\frac{\partial}{\partial q}I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})={}

\displaystyle-P^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{s}}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{s}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}x_{j}w(\mathbf{x})\bigg)e(P^{2}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))e(-P\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{u}/q)\,d\mathbf{x}

(6.2)

\displaystyle+P^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{s}}4\pi iP^{2}w(\mathbf{x})({\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))e(P^{2}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))e(-P\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{u}/q)\,d\mathbf{x},

The first term in (
6.2
) has the same form as
I_{q}
with the weight
w
replaced by
\sum_{j=1}^{s}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}x_{j}w(\mathbf{x})
, whose contribution in the integral against
p_{2}(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})
can be dealt with using first part of Lemma
6.3
.
To handle the second term in (
6.2
