), we change to radial coordinates and then remove the factor
P^{2}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x})
by integration by parts.

As in Lemma
6.3
, let
\mu_{S^{1}}
be the Lebesgue measure (arc-length measure) on the unit circle
S^{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then, multiplying (
6.2
) by
p_{2}(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})
and integrating over
|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\asymp W
, we must bound

\displaystyle\int_{C_{1}W\leq|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\leq C_{2}W}p_{2}(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})P^{s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{s}}4\pi iP^{2}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x})w(\mathbf{x})e(-P\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{u}/q)\,d\mathbf{x}\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{1}}\int_{C_{1}W}^{C_{2}W}tP^{s}p_{2}(q,t{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{s}}4\pi itP^{2}{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x})w(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times e(P^{2}t{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))e(-P\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{u}/q)\,d\mathbf{x}\,dt\,d\mu_{S^{1}}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}}).

Using integration by parts with respect to
t
(and the product rule on the resulting derivative), this is equal to

2P^{s}t^{2}(B-I_{1}-I_{2})
, where

B=\int_{S^{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{s}}p_{2}(q,t{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})w(\mathbf{x})e(P^{2}t{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))e(-P\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{u}/q)\,d\mathbf{x}\ \Big|_{C_{1}W}^{C_{2}W}d\mu_{S^{1}}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}}),

and

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=2\int_{S^{1}}\int_{C_{1}W}^{C_{2}W}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{s}}t^{-1}p_{2}(q,t{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})w(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times e(P^{2}t{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))e(-P\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{u}/q)\,d\mathbf{x}\,dt\,d\mu_{S^{1}}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}}),

and

I_{2}
is given by

\int_{S^{1}}\int_{C_{1}W}^{C_{2}W}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{s}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p_{2}(q,t{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})\right)w(\mathbf{x})e(P^{2}t{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))e(-P\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{u}/q)\,d\mathbf{x}\,dt\,d\mu_{S^{1}}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}}).

The second term is satisfactory by the first part of Lemma
6.3
, and the other terms are satisfactory by the second part of Lemma
6.3
.
∎

7.
Bounds for quadratic exponential sums

In this section, we obtain bounds for two types of quadratic exponential sums: the sums
D_{q}(\mathbf{u})
in (
5.6
) and
S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})
in (
5.7
). The exponential sums
D_{q}(\mathbf{u})
are closely related to
S_{d,1}(\mathbf{u})
, defined in
[
6
, eq. (1.2)]
. The exponential sums
S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})
are closely related to the exponential sums studied in
[
37
]
in the function field setting. We will adapt the methods in
[
6
]
and
[
37
]
to study these sums in our setting.

Using the Chinese remainder theorem, we have the following multiplicativity properties for
D_{q}
and
S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
s,q_{1},q_{2},d_{1},d_{2}\geq 1
be integers satisfying
\gcd(q_{1},q_{2})=1
and
d_{1}\mid q_{1},d_{2}\mid q_{2}
. Then for any primitive integer vector
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and any integer vector
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}
, we have the following multiplicativity relations:

\displaystyle D_{q_{1}q_{2}}(\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=D_{q_{1}}(\mathbf{u})D_{q_{2}}(\mathbf{u}),

\displaystyle S_{q_{1}q_{2},d_{1}d_{2}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}

\displaystyle=S_{q_{1},d_{1}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})S_{q_{2},d_{2}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})

7.1.
Bounds for
D_{q}(\mathbf{u})

In this subsection, our aim is to prove Lemma
7.5
. This is essentially a well-known estimate for for
D_{q}
. In addition to handling
N_{0}(P,\delta)
and
N_{1}(P,\delta)
, a special case of this result is gives Lemma
7.12
in the next subsection, which plays an important role for the contributions from bad paris of
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
to
N_{2}(P,\delta)
.

Using the multiplicativity in Lemma
7.1
, it is enough to consider the case when
q=p^{k}
where
p
is prime and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Lemma 7.2
.

For
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}
we have

\displaystyle D_{p}(\mathbf{u})\ll\begin{cases}p^{(s+2)/2}&p\nmid{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}),\\
p^{(s+3)/2}&p\mid{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}),p\nmid\mathbf{u}.\end{cases}

Here,
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})
denotes the dual variety for the intersection of quadrics
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
defined in Section
5.3
.

Proof.

We write

\displaystyle D_{p}(\mathbf{u})=\sum_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod p}\sum_{\mathbf{b}\bmod p}e_{p}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{b})+\mathbf{b}\cdot\mathbf{u})-\sum_{\mathbf{b}\bmod p}e_{p}(\mathbf{b}\cdot\mathbf{u}).

The lemma follows from
[
6
, Lemma 19]
since the second sum vanishes unless
p\mid\mathbf{u}
.
∎

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
k\geq 2,\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}
, we have
D_{p^{k}}(\mathbf{u})=0
unless
p\mid D_{F}\mathscr{F}^{*}(\mathbf{u})
.

Proof.

We write

\displaystyle D_{p^{k}}(\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{p^{k}}}\sum_{\mathbf{b}\bmod p}e_{p^{k}}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{b})+\mathbf{b}\cdot\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle\quad-p^{s}\mathds{1}_{p\mid\mathbf{u}}\Big(\sum_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{p^{k-1}}}\sum_{\mathbf{b}\bmod p^{k-1}}e_{p^{k-1}}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{b})+\mathbf{b}\cdot\frac{\mathbf{u}}{p})\Big).

If
p\nmid D_{F}\mathscr{F}^{*}(\mathbf{u})
, then
p\nmid\mathbf{u}
and the lemma then follows from an application of Hensel’s lemma akin to
[
6
, Lemma 20]
.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

For any
q\geq 1
and
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}
, we have

D_{q}(\mathbf{u})\ll q^{s/2+2+\varepsilon}
for any
\varepsilon>0
.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.1
, it is enough to check the bound for
q=p^{k}
for some integer
k
. After a standard squaring argument such as
[
37
, Lemma 2.5]
,

|\sum_{\mathbf{b}\bmod q}e_{q}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{b})+\mathbf{b}\cdot\mathbf{u})|\ll q^{s/2}Z(\vec{\mathrm{a}};q)^{1/2},

where
Z(\vec{\mathrm{a}};q)=\#\{\mathbf{z}\bmod q:q\mid\mathbf{z}^{T}(a_{1}M_{1}+a_{2}M_{2}),\}

where
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are matrices defining the quadratic forms. As a result,

\displaystyle|D_{p^{r}}(\mathbf{u})|

\displaystyle\ll p^{ks/2}\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod p^{k}}Z(\vec{\mathrm{a}};p^{k})^{1/2}\ll p^{ks/2+k}(\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod p^{k}}Z(\vec{\mathrm{a}};p^{k}))^{1/2}

\displaystyle\ll p^{ks/2+2k+\varepsilon}=q^{s/2+2+\varepsilon},

for any
\varepsilon>0

upon using
[
16
, Lemma 5.4]
.
∎

Combining Lemmas
7.2
–
7.4
, we obtain the following upper bound for
D_{q}(\mathbf{u})
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Suppose
q=q_{1}q_{2}
where
q_{1}
is the square-free part of
q
and
q_{2}
is the square-full part of
q
. Then for any
\varepsilon>0

D_{q}(\mathbf{u})\ll q^{s/2+1+\varepsilon}\gcd(q_{1},{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}))^{1/2}\gcd(q_{1},\mathbf{u})^{1/2}\gcd(q_{2},(D_{F}{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}))^{\infty}).

7.2.
Bounds for
S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})

Recall that the sum
S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})
was defined in (
5.7
).
The function field analogue of similar exponential sums defined in
[
37
, eq. (6.1)]
have been extensively studied there.
When
(d,q)=1
, the exponential sum
S_{q,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})
coincides with that considered in
[
37
, Section 6]
in the function field setting and analogous estimates can be proved similarly. By Lemma
7.1
, it is enough to consider sums
S_{p^{k},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})
. In the next four lemmas, we will obtain estimates for
S_{p^{k},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}
when
p
is a good prime.

Lemma 7.6

(Type I primes for good
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
)
.

Let
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
be good.
For
p\nmid 2\det M_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}
, we have

\displaystyle|S_{p^{k},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|\leq\begin{cases}p^{sk/2}\gcd(F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}),p^{k})&2\mid s\text{ or }2\nmid s,2\mid k,\\
p^{k(s+1)/2}\gcd(F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}),p^{k})^{1/2}\mathds{1}_{F_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\not=0}&2\nmid s,2\nmid k.\end{cases}

Proof.

Since
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is primitive, we see that
p^{k}\mid{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{a}}^{\perp}
in (
5.7
) implies that
\vec{\mathrm{a}}=a{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
for some
(a,p)=1
so that (
5.7
) can be written as

\displaystyle S_{p^{k},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{a\bmod p^{k}}\sum_{\mathbf{b}\bmod{p^{k}}}e_{p^{k}}(a{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{b})+\mathbf{b}\cdot\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{a\bmod p^{k}}\sum_{\mathbf{b}\bmod{p^{k}}}e_{p^{k}}(aF_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{b})+\mathbf{b}\cdot\mathbf{u}).

From
[
6
, Lemma 15]
, we have
for
p\nmid 2\det M_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}

(7.1)

\displaystyle S_{p^{k},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})=\begin{cases}p^{sk/2}\varepsilon(p)^{sk}\chi_{p}(\det M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{k}c_{p^{k}}(F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}))&2\mid s,\\
p^{sk/2}c_{p^{k}}(F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}))&2\nmid s,2\mid k,\\
p^{sk/2}\varepsilon(p)^{s}\chi_{p}(-1)g_{p^{k}}(F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}))&2\nmid s,2\nmid k,\end{cases}

where

\varepsilon(p)=\begin{cases}1&p\equiv 1\bmod 4\\
i&p\equiv 3\bmod 4,\end{cases}

\chi_{p}(\cdot)=(\frac{\cdot}{p})
is the Legendre Symbol,
F_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}^{*}
defined in (
5.9
) is the adjoint quadratic form of
F_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}
,
c_{p^{k}}(a)=\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{x\bmod{p^{k}}}e_{p^{k}}(ax)
is the Ramanujan sum, and

g_{p^{k}}(a)=\sum_{x\bmod{p^{k}}}\chi_{p}(x)e_{p^{k}}(ax)
is the Gauss sum.
The lemma follows from the bounds
|c_{p^{k}}(a)|\leq(a,p^{k})
and
|g_{p^{k}}(a)|\leq p^{k/2}(a,p^{k})^{1/2}\mathds{1}_{a\not=0}
.
∎

While dealing with a pair of quadrics over
s=9
variables, we will need to make use of cancellations over averages of the exponential sums
S_{q,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})
over
q
, which is the content of the following lemma.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
s
be an odd integer,
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be a good pair, and let
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}
be such that
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})
is not a perfect square. Assume the generalized Lindelöf Hypothesis for Dirichlet
L
-functions. Then for any integer
\mathfrak{B}
such that
2\det M_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}\mid\mathfrak{B}
and any
\varepsilon>0
, we have that

|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq q\leq x\\
\gcd(q,\mathfrak{B})=1\end{subarray}}S_{q,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|\ll(\mathfrak{B}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}||\mathbf{u}|)^{\varepsilon}x^{s/2+1+\varepsilon}.

Proof.

This can be viewed as a conditional version of
[
6
, Lemma 18]
and can be proved in the same way. Let

\xi_{\mathfrak{B}}(z,\mathbf{u})=\prod_{p\nmid\mathfrak{B}}\Big(\varepsilon(p)^{s}\chi_{p}(-1)\sum_{2\nmid k}\frac{g_{p^{k}}(F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}))}{p^{k(z-s/2)}}+\sum_{2\mid k}\frac{c_{p^{k}}(F_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})}{p^{k(z-s/2)}}\Big).

Since
g_{p}(a)=\chi_{p}(a)\varepsilon(p)p^{1/2}
, we see that

\xi_{\mathfrak{B}}(z,\mathbf{u})=L(z-\frac{s+1}{2},\psi_{\mathbf{u}})E_{\mathfrak{B}}(z),

where
\psi_{\mathbf{u}}(\cdot)=\Big(\frac{(-1)^{\frac{s+1}{2}}F_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})}{\cdot}\Big)
is a Dirichlet character with conductor of size
O(|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|^{s-1}|\mathbf{u}|^{2})
and
E_{\mathfrak{B}}(z)
is an Euler product, which is absolutely convergent when
\Re(z)>s/2+1
and satisfies the bound
E_{\mathfrak{B}}(z)\ll{\mathfrak{B}}^{\varepsilon}
, for any
\varepsilon>0
. Thus
\xi_{\mathfrak{B}}(z,\mathbf{u})
can be analytic continued to the region
\Re(z)>s/2+1
. Using Perron’s formula, and the identity (
7.1
) which we quoted from
[
6
, Lemma 15]
, we have for
c>s/2+2

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq q\leq x\\
(q,\mathfrak{B})=1\end{subarray}}S_{q,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{c-iT}^{c+iT}\xi_{\mathfrak{B}}(z,\mathbf{u})\frac{x^{z}}{z}dz+O(\frac{x^{c}}{T}).

Moving the line of integration to
s/2+1+\varepsilon
, using the bound
\xi_{\mathfrak{B}}(z,\mathbf{u})\ll(|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|^{s-1}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}\mathfrak{B}T)^{\varepsilon}
, and taking
T
to be a sufficiently large power of
x
, we obtain the result.
∎

Lemma 7.8

(Type II primes for good
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
)
.

Let
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
be good. Let
M_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}=TDS
where
T,S\in GL_{s}(\mathbb{Z})
and
D={\mathrm{diag}}(\rho_{i})
with
\rho_{i}\mid\rho_{i+1}
.
Suppose
p\nmid D_{F}
and
p\mid\det(M_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}})
and let
k_{1}=\min(k,\nu_{p}(\rho_{s}))
, where for any integer
n
,
\nu_{p}(n)
is the largest integer
k
such that
p^{k}\mid n
. Then

S_{p^{k}}({\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u})

is non-zero unless
p^{k_{1}}\mid((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}
, and we also have

\displaystyle|S_{p^{k},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|\ll p^{k(s/2+1)}\gcd(p^{k_{1}},Q_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}),((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s})^{1/2},

where
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
denotes the
s-1
dimensional vector by deleting the
s
-th entry of
(S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u}
.

Proof.

This can be proved in the same way as the function field analogue
[
37
, Lemma 6.3]
.
∎

We now move on to bad pairs
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
. In this case, the matrix
M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is singular and therefore the last diagonal entry
\rho_{s}=0
in the Smith normal decomposition of
M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
as stated before. We also have

F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathbf{x})=Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}),

where as before
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
denotes the
s-1
dimensional vector by deleting the
s
-th entry of
(S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u}
.

Lemma 7.9

(Good primes for bad
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
)
.

Let
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
be bad. Then for a good prime
p
, we have
S_{p^{k},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})
vanishes unless
p^{k}\mid((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}
, and we have

\displaystyle|S_{p^{k},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|

\displaystyle\ll p^{k}\mathds{1}_{p^{k}\mid((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}}\begin{cases}p^{k(s/2-1/2)}\gcd(p^{k},Q_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}))&\textrm{ if }2\mid k\text{ or }2\nmid k,2\nmid s,\\
p^{ks/2}&\textrm{ if }2\nmid k,2\mid s.\end{cases}

Proof.

This can be proved in the same way as the function field analogue
[
37
, Lemma 6.4]
.
∎

Next, we consider general bounds for
S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}
with
(q,d)>1
. When
(d,q)>1
, the definition of
S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})
differ slightly from that in
[
37
, Section 6]
due to the co-primality condition in
L(d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})
defined in
[
37
]
, but the same bounds apply by following the proofs in
[
37
, Lemma 6.5, Lemma 6.7]
. By Lemma
7.1
, it is again enough to consider
S_{p^{k},p^{m}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}
with
1\leq m\leq k.
We start with the following lemma corresponding to
[
37
, Lemma 6.7]
, which will be used to obtain a general bound for
S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}
.

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be primitive. For integers
1\leq m\leq k
, denote
k_{1}=\min(k-m,\nu_{p}(\det M_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}))
, where
\min(k-m,\infty)=k-m
by convention. Then we have

\displaystyle|S_{p^{k},p^{m}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|\ll_{D_{F}}p^{k(n/2+1)+m+k_{1}/2}\mathds{1}_{p^{k_{1}}\mid((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}}.

Proof.

Akin to
[
37
, Lemma 3.3]
, we see that

\displaystyle\{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{p^{k}}:(\vec{\mathrm{a}},p)=1,p^{k}\mid p^{m}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{a}}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{a{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{\perp}+p^{k-m}\vec{\mathrm{d}}\bmod{p^{k}}:(a,p)=1,a\bmod p^{k-m},\vec{\mathrm{d}}\bmod{p^{m}}\},

so that we can write

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{p^{k}}\\
(\vec{\mathrm{a}},p)=1\\
p^{k-m}\mid\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\mathbf{b}\bmod{p^{k}}}e_{p^{k}}(\vec{\mathrm{a}}\cdot F(\mathbf{b})+\mathbf{b}\cdot\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\vec{\mathrm{d}}\bmod{p^{m}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\bmod{p^{k-m}}\\
(a,p)=1\end{subarray}}e_{p^{k}}((a{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{\perp}+p^{k-m}\vec{\mathrm{d}})\cdot F(\mathbf{b})+\mathbf{b}\cdot\mathbf{u}).

Then the proof follows the same way as
[
37
, Lemma 6.7]
, using that uniformly for
(a,p)=1
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\sum_{\vec{\mathrm{d}}\bmod{p^{m}}}\operatorname{gcd}(F(a{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{\perp}+p^{k-m}\vec{\mathrm{d}}),p^{k})^{1/2}\ll p^{2m+k_{1}/2},

since
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is primitive so that
(a{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{\perp}+p^{k-m}\vec{\mathrm{d}},p)=1
for all
(a,p)=1
and
\vec{\mathrm{d}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
∎

As a corollary of Lemma
7.10
, we obtain the following analogue of
[
37
, Lemma 6.8]
.

Lemma 7.11

(general bound)
.

For
d\mid q
, we have

\displaystyle|S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|

\displaystyle\ll_{D_{F}}dq^{s/2+1}\gcd(q/d,((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s},\det M_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}})^{1/2}

\displaystyle\ll_{{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}}d^{1/2}q^{s/2+3/2}.

To estimate the contribution from bad pairs
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
, we need the following refined estimate, that saves a factor of order
O(d^{1/2})
as compared to the bound in Lemma
7.11
for square-free moduli, analogous to
[
37
, Lemma 6.5]
.

Lemma 7.12
.

Let
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
be a bad pair. Then

\displaystyle|S_{p,p{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|\ll p^{s/2+1}(p,\mathbf{u})^{1/2}\gcd(p,{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}))^{1/2}.

Proof.

Since

S_{p,p{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})=D_{p}(\mathbf{u})
, this lemma follows from Lemma
7.5
.
∎

8.
Major arcs contribution: the main term
N_{0}(P,\delta)

In this section we prove the first part of Lemma
5.3
(
a
), the asymptotic formula for the main contribution
N_{0}(P,\delta)
which was defined in (
5.3
).

First we show that only
\mathbf{u}={\text{\boldmath$0$}}
contribute to the main term in
N_{0}(P,\delta)
.

Lemma 8.1
.

For any
\delta,N>0
, we have

N_{0}(P,\delta)=\sum_{1\leq q<Q^{1/2-\delta}}q^{-s}D_{q}({\text{\boldmath$0$}})\int_{{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathfrak{M}_{q}(\delta)}I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},{\text{\boldmath$0$}})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}+O_{N}(P^{-N}).

Proof.

Note that for
Q=P^{4/3}
,
q\leq Q^{1/2-\delta}
, and any
{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathfrak{M}_{q}(\delta)
, we have

\frac{q}{P}(1+P^{2}|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|)\ll\frac{q}{P}(1+P^{2}\frac{1}{qQ^{1+\delta}})\ll\frac{q}{P}+\frac{P}{Q}\ll P^{-1/3}.

Thus from the first part of Lemma
6.1
, we conclude that the contributions from all non-zero
\mathbf{u}
are negligible in
N_{0}(P,\delta)
. ∎

To further evaluate
N_{0}(P,\delta)
, we define the singular series

(8.1)

\mathfrak{S}:=\sum_{q=1}^{\infty}q^{-s}D_{q}({\text{\boldmath$0$}})

and the singular integral
\mathfrak{J}

(8.2)

\mathfrak{J}:=\int J({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\text{ where }J({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})=\int w(\mathbf{x})e({\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))d\mathbf{x}.

Lemma 8.2
.

The singular integral
\mathfrak{J}
is absolutely convergent as soon as
s\geq 6
and the singular series
\mathfrak{S}
is absolutely convergent for any
s\geq 7
. Moreover, for any
s\geq 7
and any
\varepsilon>0
, we have

(8.3)

N_{0}(P,\delta)=P^{s-4}\left(\mathfrak{SJ}+O(P^{-(s-6)/3+\varepsilon})\right)

for sufficiently small
\delta
(depending on
\varepsilon
and
s
).

Proof.

First we show the convergence of
\mathfrak{J}
and
\mathfrak{S}
.
Using Lemma
6.1
, we see that

\displaystyle J({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})\ll(1+|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|)^{-(s-1)/2},

and it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\gg W}J({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}

\displaystyle\ll\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\gg W}(1+|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|)^{-(s-1)/2}d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}

(8.4)

\displaystyle\ll\int_{r\gg W}r^{-(s-1)/2+1}dr\ll W^{-s/2+5/2}.

The singular integral is therefore absolutely convergent for
s\geq 6
.
From Lemma
7.5
,
we have the bound

(8.5)

|D_{q}({\text{\boldmath$0$}})|\ll q^{s/2+2+\varepsilon},

for any
\varepsilon>0
, which implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{q\geq 1}q^{-s}|D_{q}({\text{\boldmath$0$}})|\ll\sum_{q\geq 1}q^{-s/2+2+\varepsilon}

for any
\varepsilon>0
.
Therefore, the singular series
\mathfrak{S}
converges absolutely for all
s\geq 7
.

Next we evaluate
N_{0}(P,\delta)
.
Using Lemma
8.1

and a change of variables,

\displaystyle\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|<q^{-1}Q^{-1-\varepsilon}}I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},{\text{\boldmath$0$}})=P^{s-4}\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|<q^{-1}Q^{1/2-\delta}}J({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}},

we see that

(8.6)

N_{0}(P,\delta)=P^{s-4}\sum_{1\leq q<Q^{1/2-\delta}}q^{-s}D_{q}({\text{\boldmath$0$}})\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|<q^{-1}Q^{1/2-\delta}}J({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}+O_{N}(P^{-N}).

Using (
8.4
), we can
we can replace the integral over
{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}
by
\mathfrak{J}
with an error

O\big((q^{-1}Q^{1/2-\delta})^{-s/2+5/2}\big)=O(q^{(s-5)/2}Q^{-1+\varepsilon}),

for any
s\geq 7
, provided
\delta
is sufficiently small depending on
\varepsilon
and
s
.

Using (
8.5
), it then follows that for
s\geq 7
and for sufficiently small
\delta
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq q\leq Q^{1/2-\delta}}q^{-s}D_{q}({\text{\boldmath$0$}})\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|<q^{-1}Q^{1/2-\delta}}J({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{J}\sum_{1\leq q<Q^{1/2-\delta}}q^{-s}D_{q}({\text{\boldmath$0$}})+O\Big(\sum_{1\leq q<Q^{1/2-\delta}}q^{-s/2+2}q^{(s-5)/2}Q^{-1+\varepsilon}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{JS}+O\Big(\sum_{q\geq Q^{1/2-\delta}}q^{-s/2+2+\varepsilon}+Q^{-1+\varepsilon}\sum_{q\leq Q^{1/2-\delta}}q^{-1/2}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{JS}+O(Q^{-(s-6)/4+\varepsilon}+Q^{-3/4+\varepsilon}))=\mathfrak{JS}+O(P^{-(s-6)/3+\varepsilon}),

which together with (
8.6
) yields (
8.3
).
∎

9.
Counting estimates: dimension growth and congruences

In this section, we prepare some lemmas to estimate the contributions from the
\mathbf{u}
-sum and
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
-sum. Our first estimate is essentially the uniform version of the dimension growth theorem of Salberger
[
34
, Theorem 0.3]
, however we will use an affine version.

Lemma 9.1

(Dimension growth)
.

Let
X\subset\mathbb{P}^{s-1}
be an irreducible variety over
\mathbb{Q}
satisfying
\dim X\geq 1
,
\deg X\geq 2
and
s\geq 3
. Let

\eta(d)=\begin{cases}0&(d\neq 3),\\
\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}-1&(d=3).\end{cases}

Let
\mathscr{Y}\subset\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{s}
be an integral model of the affine cone over
X
. Then for
V\geq 1
and any
\varepsilon>0

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{Y}(\mathbb{Q})\cap\mathbb{Z}^{s}:|\mathbf{x}|\leq V\}\ll_{\deg X,s,\varepsilon}V^{\operatorname{dim}X+\eta(\deg X)+\varepsilon}.

We remark that at one point, namely (
13.11
), we need very slightly more than the last proposition in the degree 2 case, and draw on
[
37
, Lemma 3.7]
.

Proof.

Let
d=\deg X
.
When
d\geq 3
, Salberger proved
[
34
, Theorem 0.3]
that

(9.1)

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{Y}(\mathbb{Q})\cap\mathbb{Z}^{s}:\gcd(\mathbf{x})=1,|\mathbf{x}|\leq V\}\ll_{d,s,\varepsilon}V^{\operatorname{dim}X+\eta(d)+\varepsilon}.

Salberger states this for integral varieties; recall that for us all varieties are reduced, and thus integral and irreducible are interchangeable terms.

We prove (
9.1
) in the case
d=2
. Suppose first that
X
is geometrically reducible. In that case
X
is a union of two hyperplanes conjugate over a quadratic extension of
\mathbb{Q}
. All the points in
X(\mathbb{Q})
lie on the intersection of these hyperplanes, which is a projective linear space of dimension
\dim X-1
; thus
\mathscr{Y}(\mathbb{Q})
is contained in an affine linear space of dimension
\dim X
and therefore makes an acceptable contribution to the counting function in the lemma. In the remaining case when
X
is geometrically irreducible, (
9.1
) is proved in Heath-Brown
[
15
, Theorem 2]
.

We now need to remove the
\gcd
-condition in the above estimate. Clearly, using (
9.1
),

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{Y}(\mathbb{Q})\cap\mathbb{Z}^{s}:|\mathbf{x}|\leq V\}\ll\sum_{1\leq m\leq V}(V/m)^{\dim X+\varepsilon}\ll V^{\dim X+\eta(\deg X)+\varepsilon},

as soon as
\dim X\geq 1
.
∎

We shall apply the following lemma to control the dimensions of a type of varieties appearing in our analysis.

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be a good, primitive integer vector, suppose
s\geq 4
and let
X\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{s-1}
be the variety defined by
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=F^{*}_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathbf{u})=0
. Then

(i)

X
is a complete intersection of dimension
s-3
defined over
\mathbb{Q}
.

(ii)

X
does not contain any linear space of projective dimension
\geq\lfloor\frac{s}{2}\rfloor
.

(iii)

If
s\geq 7
then
X
does not have any component of degree
3
.

Proof.

For part (i), it suffices to show that
F^{*}_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}=0
and
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}=0
do not have a common irreducible component over
{\mathbb{Q}}
. But every component of
F^{*}_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}=0
has degree
\leq 2
, while
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}=0
is irreducible with degree
4(s-2)\geq 4
, so we are done.

For part (ii), observe that
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is good, so
F^{*}_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is a quadratic form of rank
s
. By Witt’s Theorem, the maximum possible index of isotropy of
F^{*}_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is
\lfloor\frac{s}{2}\rfloor
, that is

F^{*}_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}=0
cannot contain a linear space of
projective
dimension
>\lfloor\frac{s}{2}\rfloor-1
and
a fortiori
the same is true of
X
.

For (iii), suppose
V\subset\mathbb{P}^{s-1}
is such a component.
Then
\deg V=1+\operatorname{codim}V
, making
V
a
variety of minimal degree
. As
V
has degree 3 it is a cone over a rational normal scroll
[
12
, Theorem 1]
. In particular there are linear spaces
L\subseteq V
with codimension 1
[
12
, p5]
. Now by the previous part (ii), we have
\dim(V)-1<\lfloor\frac{s}{2}\rfloor
, while by part (i) we have
\dim(V)=s-3
. This implies
s<7
.
∎

Remark 9.3
.

The proof of (iii) is inspired by the mathoverflow answer of Sasha
[
35
]
, which would actually permit us to weaken the hypothesis in (iii) to
s\geq 6
by a closer inspection of the possibilities for
V
at the end of our argument. Presumably (ii) is not optimal either, but it is enough for our purposes.

We will also need the following counting lemma generalizing
[
37
, Lemma 8.1]
.

Lemma 9.4

(Counting lemma)
.

For any
s\geq 2
,
q,d\in\mathbb{N}
with
d\mid q
, any primitive
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and any
V\geq 1
, we have that

\displaystyle\#\{|\mathbf{u}|\leq V:q\mid((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s},d\mid Q_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})\}

(9.2)

\displaystyle\ll V^{s-2}\min\Big(V(1+\frac{V}{q}),(1+\frac{V}{\prod_{p\mid d}p})(1+\frac{V}{\prod_{p\mid q}p})\Big),

and for
N\geq 0
,
x\in\mathbb{N}
and any
\varepsilon>0
,

(9.3)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\textrm{ good }\\
|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
x\mid\det M_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}\end{subarray}}(1+N\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}\ll_{F,\varepsilon}x^{\varepsilon}C\Big(1+\frac{C}{x^{1/2}(1+N)^{1/2}}\Big),

where
\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is as defined in (
5.10
).

Proof.

Under the assumptions of the lemma, equation (
9.2
) can be proved the same way as its function field analogue in
[
37
, eq. (8.6)]
and we omit the details here.

Equation (
9.3
) is a generalization of
[
37
, eq. (8.7)]
. We show that for any
x_{1}\mid x
satisfying
x_{1}\geq x^{1/2}

(9.4)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\textrm{ good }\\
|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
x\mid\det M_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}\\
\gcd(x_{1},c_{1})=1\end{subarray}}(1+N\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}\ll_{F,\varepsilon}x^{\varepsilon}C\Big(1+\frac{C}{x_{1}(1+N)^{1/2}}\Big),

which implies (
9.3
).

Fix a value of
c_{1}
co-prime to
x_{1}
. Following the proof of
[
37
, eq. (8.7)]
, one can show that there exist integers
0\leq b_{1},...,b_{K}<x_{1}
where
K=O_{D_{F}}(x_{1}^{\varepsilon})
such that every
|c_{2}|\ll C
satisfying
\gcd(c_{1},c_{2})=1
and
x_{1}\mid\det(M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})
must be of the form
b_{i}+kx_{1}
for some
|k|\ll 1+C/x_{1}
. Therefore

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\textrm{ good }\\
|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
x\mid\det M_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}\\
\gcd(c_{1},x_{1})=1\end{subarray}}(1+N\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|c_{1}|\ll C\\
\gcd(c_{1},x)=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{j=1}^{K}\sum_{|k|\ll 1+C/x_{1}}(1+N\lambda_{(c_{1},b_{j}+kx_{1})})^{-1/2}.

Now, we have uniformly for any
0\leq b<x_{1}
and
c_{1}

\displaystyle\sum_{|k|\ll C/x_{1}}(1+N\lambda_{(c_{1},b+kx_{1})})^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\ll 1+\sum_{0\not=|k|\ll C/x_{1}}(1+Nkx_{1}/C)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\ll 1+\sum_{0\not=|k|\leq\frac{C}{x_{1}(1+N)}}1+\sum_{\frac{C}{x_{1}(1+N)}\ll|k|\ll\frac{C}{x_{1}}}C^{1/2}N^{-1/2}x_{1}^{-1/2}k^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\ll 1+\frac{C}{x_{1}(1+N)^{1/2}},

which is enough to establish (
9.4
), upon recalling that
K=O_{D_{F}}(x^{\varepsilon})
.
∎

10.
Minor arcs contribution: the term
N_{1}(P,\delta)

The objective of this section is to prove the second bound claimed in Lemma
5.3
(
b
), concerning the quantity

N_{1}(P,\delta)
defined in (
5.4
),
when
\delta
is sufficiently small depending on
\varepsilon
and
s
.

10.1.
First reductions.

From Lemmas
5.2
and
6.1
, we see that

\displaystyle N_{1}(P,\delta)+O_{N,\varepsilon}(P^{-N})

(10.1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq q\leq Q}q^{-s}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}\\
|\mathbf{u}|\leq\frac{q}{P}(1+P^{2}|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|)P^{\varepsilon}\end{subarray}}D_{q}(\mathbf{u})\int_{\mathfrak{m}_{q}(\delta)}p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}},

for any
\varepsilon,N>0
. Although we write
O_{N,\varepsilon}
in the line above for clarity, we recall before taking the next step that our implicit constants can always depend on
s,w,F_{1},F_{2}
and
\varepsilon
, and that
\varepsilon
can vary between occurrences.
Moreover we split
q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}
into dyadic ranges
q\asymp Y,|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\asymp W
, we see that
\mathbf{u}
can be truncated up to
V
,
where

(10.2)

V=\frac{Y}{P}(1+P^{2}W)P^{\varepsilon}.

In view of the minor arcs
\mathfrak{m}_{q}(\delta)
defined in (
5.1
), we introduce the set
\mathscr{C}_{\delta}
as

(10.3)

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{\delta}=\Big\{(Y,W):1\leq Y\leq Q^{1/2-\delta},\frac{Q^{-\delta}}{YQ}\ll W\ll\frac{Q^{\delta}}{YQ^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\text{or }Q^{1/2-\delta}\leq Y\leq Q,W\ll\frac{Q^{\delta}}{YQ^{1/2}}\Big\}.

Every pair
(q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})
occurring in (
10.1
) satisfies
Y\leq q\leq 2Y,W\leq|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\leq 2W
for some
(Y,W)\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}
. Therefore we have

\displaystyle N_{1}(P,\delta)

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{(Y,W)\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}}\bigg\lvert\sum_{q\asymp Y}q^{-s}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}\\
|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\end{subarray}}D_{q}(\mathbf{u})\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\asymp W}p_{1,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\bigg\rvert+P^{-N},

where, by a slight abuse of notation, we embrace both of the conditions
W\leq|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\leq 2W,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathfrak{m}_{q}(\delta)
in the notation
|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\asymp W
, so that the implicit constants are always in
[1,2]
, and are usually 1 and 2, but may differ when
W
is almost as large or as small as possible.

Writing
q=q_{1}q_{2}
where
q_{1}
is square free,
q_{2}
is square-full with
q_{1}\asymp Y_{1},q_{2}\asymp Y_{2}
and
Y_{1}Y_{2}\asymp Y
, we can apply
Lemma
6.2
to the
{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}
-integral and Lemma
7.5
to
D_{q}(\mathbf{u})
to obtain the bound

\displaystyle N_{1}(P,\delta)

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y_{1}Y_{2}\asymp Y\\
(Y,W)\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}\end{subarray}}Y^{-s/2}QW^{2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-s/2-1}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{q_{i}\asymp Y_{i}}\sum_{|\mathbf{u}|\leq V}\gcd({\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}),q_{1})^{1/2}\gcd(\mathbf{u},q_{1})^{1/2}\gcd(q_{2},(D_{F}{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}))^{\infty}).

To estimate the
\mathbf{u}
-sum, we consider the contribution from
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\not=0
and
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0
separately.

10.2.
Case I:
\mathscr{F}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\not=0

The contributions from
\mathscr{F}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\not=0
can be bounded by

\displaystyle P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y_{1}Y_{2}\asymp Y\\
(Y,W)\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}\end{subarray}}Y^{-s/2}QW^{2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-s/2-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{q_{i}\asymp Y_{i}}

\displaystyle\gcd({\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}),q_{1})^{1/2}\gcd(\mathbf{u},q_{1})^{1/2}\gcd(q_{2},(D_{F}{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}))^{\infty})

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y_{1}Y_{2}\asymp Y\\
(Y,W)\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}\end{subarray}}Y^{-s/2}QW^{2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-s/2-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\neq 0\end{subarray}}Y_{1}Y_{2}

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}(Y,W)\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}\end{subarray}}Y^{1-s/2}QW^{2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-s/2-1}V^{s}

(10.4)

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}(Y,W)\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}\end{subarray}}Y^{1+s/2}QW^{2}(1+P^{2}W)^{s/2-1}.

The right hand side of (
10.4
) is maximized when
W\asymp Y^{-1}Q^{-1/2+\delta}
and
Y\asymp Q
, which is

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}Y^{1+s/2}QW^{2}(Q/Y)^{s/2-1}Q^{(s/2-1)\delta}\ll P^{\varepsilon}Q^{s/2-1+(s/2+1)\delta}.

By choosing
\delta
sufficiently small (depending on
\varepsilon
and
s
),
this contribution is bounded by

\displaystyle Q^{2+s/2+\varepsilon}P^{-4}\ll P^{s-4-(s-8)/3+\varepsilon},

handing us the bound in Lemma
5.3
(
b
).

10.3.
Case II:
\mathscr{F}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0

To deal with the contribution from
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0
terms, we use Lemma
9.1
to obtain

\#\{|\mathbf{u}|\leq V:{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0,\mathbf{u}\neq{\text{\boldmath$0$}}\}\ll V^{s-2+\varepsilon}.

The contribution from
\mathscr{F}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0
terms is therefore bounded by

\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y_{1}Y_{2}\asymp Y\\
(Y,W)\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}\end{subarray}}Y^{-s/2}QW^{2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-s/2-1}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{q_{i}\asymp Y_{i}}\gcd({\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}),q_{1})^{1/2}\gcd(\mathbf{u},q_{1})^{1/2}\gcd(q_{2},(D_{F}{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}))^{\infty})

\displaystyle\ll\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y_{1}Y_{2}\asymp Y\\
(Y,W)\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}\end{subarray}}Y^{-s/2+\varepsilon}QW^{2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-s/2-1}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\sum_{q_{i}\asymp Y_{i}}\Big(Y_{1}Y_{2}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0\\
\mathbf{u}\neq{\text{\boldmath$0$}}\end{subarray}}(\mathbf{u},q_{1})^{1/2}Y_{1}^{1/2}Y_{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\ll\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y_{1}Y_{2}\asymp Y\\
(Y,W)\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}\end{subarray}}Y^{-s/2+\varepsilon}Y_{1}^{1/2}Y_{2}QW^{2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-s/2-1}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\Big(Y_{1}^{1/2}\sum_{q_{i}\asymp Y_{i}}1+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0\\
\mathbf{u}\neq{\text{\boldmath$0$}}\end{subarray}}\sum_{q_{i}\asymp Y_{i}}(\mathbf{u},q_{1})^{1/2}\Big)

\displaystyle\ll\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y_{1}Y_{2}\asymp Y\\
(Y,W)\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}\end{subarray}}P^{\varepsilon}Y^{-s/2}Y_{1}^{1/2}Y_{2}QW^{2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-s/2-1}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\Big(Y_{1}^{3/2}Y_{2}^{1/2}+V^{s-2}Y_{1}Y_{2}^{1/2}\Big)

\displaystyle\ll\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}(Y,W)\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}\end{subarray}}Y^{2-\frac{s}{2}}QW^{2}P^{s+\varepsilon}(1+P^{2}W)^{-\frac{s}{2}-1}

(10.5)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+Y^{\frac{s}{2}-1/2}QW^{2}P^{2+\varepsilon}(1+P^{2}W)^{\frac{s}{2}-3}.

Since
s\geq 5
, the first term in (
10.5
) is maximal when
Y\asymp 1,W\asymp Q^{-1-\delta}
or
Y\asymp Q^{1/2-\delta},W\asymp Q^{-3/2+\delta}\asymp P^{-2+\delta}
, which is bounded by

\displaystyle\ll Q^{-1}P^{s+\varepsilon}(P^{2}/Q)^{-s/2-1}Q^{(s/2+1)\delta}+Q^{2-s/4}P^{s-4+\varepsilon}Q^{(s/2+1)\delta}

\displaystyle\ll P^{s-4-(s-8)/3+\varepsilon}Q^{(s/2+1)\delta}

The second term in (
10.5
) is maximal when
W\asymp Y^{-1}Q^{-1/2+\delta}
and further when
Y\asymp Q
, as the resulting power of
Y
in this expression is positive. Therefore, we have,
Y\asymp Q,W\asymp Q^{-3/2+\delta}\asymp P^{-2+\delta}
, which gives a bound

\displaystyle\ll Q^{s/2+1/2+\varepsilon}P^{-4+2+\varepsilon}Q^{(s/2-1)\delta}\ll P^{s-4-(s-8)/3+\varepsilon}Q^{(s/2-1)\delta}.

By choosing
\delta
sufficiently small, the contributions from
\mathscr{F}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0
also satisfy the bound in Lemma
5.3
(
b
).

11.
Minor arcs contribution: reduction to
N_{2,1}(P,\delta)
and
N_{2,2}(P,\delta)

In this section, we divide up the sum
N_{2}(P,\delta)
(see (
11.2
) below) into several pieces in order to establish the bounds in Lemma
5.3
(
c
) for the term
N_{2}(P,\delta)
from (
5.5
)
when
\delta
is sufficiently small depending on
\varepsilon
and
s
.

11.1.
Splitting up
N_{2}(P,\delta)

From Lemmas
5.2
and
6.1
, we see that

N_{2}(P,\delta)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d,k\in\mathbb{N}\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\mathrm{primitive}\\
\vec{\mathrm{r}}=dk|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp Q^{1/2}\end{subarray}}\omega(\frac{\vec{\mathrm{r}}}{Q^{1/2}})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq q\leq Q/k\\
d\mid q\\
\gcd(q/d,k)=1\end{subarray}}q^{-s}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}\\
|\mathbf{u}|\leq\frac{q}{P}(1+P^{2}|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|)P^{\varepsilon}\end{subarray}}S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})\\
\int_{\mathfrak{m}_{q}}p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}+O_{\varepsilon,s,N}(P^{-N}).

After dividing
d,k,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},q,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}
into dyadic ranges, we obtain

(11.1)

\begin{split}N_{2}(P,\delta)+O_{N}(P^{-N})\ll{}&P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}KDC\asymp Q^{1/2}\\
D\ll Y\ll Q/K\\
(Y,W)\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}\end{subarray}}\bigg\lvert\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d,k\in\mathbb{N}\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\mathrm{primitive}\\
d\asymp D,k\asymp K,|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\in\mathbb{N}\\
d\mid q,q\asymp Y\\
\gcd(q/d,k)=1\end{subarray}}q^{-s}\\
&\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}\\
|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\end{subarray}}S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\asymp W}p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\bigg\lvert,\end{split}

where
\mathscr{C}_{\delta}
is defined in (
10.3
) and
V
is defined as (
10.2
). Here we again include the condition
{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathfrak{m}_{q}(\delta)
in the implicit constants in the notation
|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\asymp W
.

Remark 11.1
.

One could hope to take advantage of cancellations over the
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
-sum in the general style of Health-Brown–Pierce
[
16
]
and Northey-Vishe
[
30
]
by using Cauchy’s inequality to write

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\textrm{ primitive}\\
\vec{\mathrm{r}}=dk{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\end{subarray}}\omega\big(\frac{\vec{\mathrm{r}}}{Q^{1/2}}\big)\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}}S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})\int_{{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathfrak{m}_{q}(\delta)}p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}

\displaystyle\ll\bigg((\frac{Q^{\delta}}{dqk})^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\textrm{ primitive}\\
\vec{\mathrm{r}}=dk{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\\
|\vec{\mathrm{r}}|\asymp Q^{1/2}\end{subarray}}\int_{{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathfrak{m}_{q}(\delta)}\bigg\lvert\sum_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}}S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})\bigg\rvert^{2}\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\bigg)^{1/2}.

After bounding the integral, one will have sums of type
\sum_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{v})
, in which some extra cancellations might be obtained. We do not explore this here since the saving does not seem to be enough for
s=9
.

We write

(11.2)

\displaystyle N_{2}(P,\delta)\ll N_{2,0}(P,\delta)+N_{2,1}(P,\delta)+N_{2,2}(P,\delta)+O_{N}(P^{-N}),

where
N_{2,0}(P,\delta)
denotes the contribution on the right hand side in (
11.1
) from
V<1
, while
N_{2,1}(P,\delta)
denotes the contribution on the right hand side in (
11.1
) from
V\geq 1
and bad pairs
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
, and
N_{2,2}(P,\delta)
denotes the contributions on the right hand side in (
11.1
) from
V\geq 1
and good pairs
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
.

From the definitions of
V
and
\mathscr{C}_{\delta}
in (
10.2
), (
10.3
), we see that
the condition
V<1
implies that the pairs
(Y,W)
belong to the set
\mathscr{C}_{\delta}^{-}
defined as follows:

(11.3)

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}_{\delta}^{-}=\Big\{(Y,W):Y\ll Q^{1/2-\delta},Y^{-1}Q^{-1-\delta}\ll W\ll Y^{-1}P^{-1-\delta}

\displaystyle\text{ or }Q^{1/2-\delta}\ll Y\ll P^{1-\delta},W\ll Y^{-1}P^{-1-\delta}\Big\}.

We will deal with this contribution in the next subsection.

The situation for
V\geq 1
requires more work. To handle this case, we introduce the notation
\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}
to estimate the contributions from
\mathscr{C}_{\delta}\setminus\mathscr{C}_{\delta}^{-}
:

(11.4)

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}=\Big\{(K,D,C,R,Y,W):KDC\asymp Q^{1/2},\,D\ll R\ll Y\ll\frac{Q}{K},

\displaystyle(Y,W)\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta},K,D,C,V\geq 1\Big\}.

We first relate
N_{2,1}(P,\delta),N_{2,2}(P,\delta)
to certain averages involving
S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}
and
\Sigma
, depending on parameters drawn from the set
\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}
.

Lemma 11.2
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}
be as in (
11.4
). Then for
\delta
sufficiently small (depending on
\varepsilon
and
s
) and for
i=1,2
, we have

(11.5)

N_{2,i}(P,\delta)\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}\end{subarray}}\sum_{k\asymp K}\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\textup{ bad if }i=1\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\textup{ good if }i=2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}\\
|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\asymp R\\
\gcd(k,r/d)=1\\
d\mid r\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\infty}\end{subarray}}\\
r^{-s}|S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})\Sigma(r,Y/R,W,\mathfrak{P};k,d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u})|,

where
\mathfrak{P}=\mathfrak{P}(d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},{\vec{\mathrm{F}}})
is any non-zero integer satisfying
d\mid\mathfrak{P}
, and

\displaystyle\Sigma(r,Y_{1},W,\mathfrak{P};k,d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\asymp W}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\asymp Y_{1}\\
(q_{1},k\mathfrak{P})=1\end{subarray}}q_{1}^{-s}S_{q_{1},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})p_{2,dk{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},k,q_{1}r}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})I_{q_{1}r}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}.

Proof.

Using (
11.1
) and (
11.2
), we see that
N_{2,1}(P,\delta)
(from contributions with
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
bad and
V\geq 1
) can be bounded by

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}\sum_{k\asymp K}\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\text{ bad}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}\\
|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\end{subarray}}N_{2}(Y,W;k,d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u}),

where

\displaystyle N_{2}(Y,W;k,d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid q\asymp Y\\
\gcd(q/d,k)=1\end{subarray}}q^{-s}S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\asymp W}p_{2,dk{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}.

Here we again include the condition
{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\in\mathfrak{m}_{q}(\delta)
in the implicit constants in
|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\asymp W
.
We split
q=q_{1}r
, where
\gcd(q_{1},\mathfrak{P}k)=1
and
r\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\infty}
. Using
d\mid\mathfrak{P}
and multiplicativity of the exponential sums in Lemma
7.1
, we can write
S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})=S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})S_{q_{1},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})
with
(q_{1},\mathfrak{P})=1
and
r\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\infty}
. Since
d\mid q
, we must have
d\mid r
and the result follows by splitting
r
into dyadic ranges
r\asymp R
with
D\ll R\ll Y/K
. This completes the proof of (
11.5
) when
i=1
. The proof for
i=2
follows the same way by adjusting the conditions on
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
.
∎

In the case of
N_{2,1}
we further split the sum over
\mathbf{u}
in on the right hand of (
11.5
) into two cases:

(11.6)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\end{subarray}}(\cdots)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})\neq 0\\
\textrm{ or }((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}\neq 0\end{subarray}}(\cdots)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})=((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}=0\end{subarray}}(\cdots).

We define
E_{1,1}(P,\delta)
and
E_{1,2}(P,\delta)
, corresponding to contributions to the right hand side in (
11.5
) for bad
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
, from each of the two terms on the right hand side above. Note that when
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is bad, we have
\lambda_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}=0
.

When we are considering
N_{2,2}
, we split the sum over
\mathbf{u}
on the right hand side of (
11.5
) into two cases in a way that depends on the parity of
s
. For odd
s
, we put

(11.7)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\end{subarray}}(\cdots)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\neq 0\end{subarray}}(\cdots)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0\end{subarray}}(\cdots).

We then define sums
E_{2,1}(P,\delta)
and
E_{2,2}(P,\delta)
, corresponding to the contribution from the two terms on the right hand side above to the right hand side of (
11.5
) for good
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
, that is for
N_{2,2}
.
For even
s
,
we instead split the sum over
\mathbf{u}
on the right hand side of (
11.5
) into

(11.8)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}\\
|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\end{subarray}}(\cdots)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}\\
|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\neq\square\end{subarray}}(\cdots)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}\\
|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=\square\end{subarray}}(\cdots).

When
s
is even, then we define the sums
E_{2,1}(P,\delta)
and
E_{2,2}(P,\delta)
to be the contribution to the bound (
11.5
) for
N_{2,2}
, from the first and second terms on the right hand side of (
11.8
) respectively.

We shall obtain the following estimates for
N_{2,i}(P,\delta)
for
i=0,1,2
.

Lemma 11.3
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and adopt the notation defined above, so that

N_{2,i}\ll E_{i,1}+E_{i,2}
for
i=1,2
. Then, for sufficiently small
\delta
depending on
\varepsilon
and
s
, we have

(1)

N_{2,0}(P,\delta)\ll P^{s-4-(s-8)/3+\varepsilon}
,

(2)

\max_{i=1,2}E_{1,i}(P,\delta)\ll\begin{cases}P^{(s-4)-(s-9)/3+\varepsilon}&2\mid s,\\
P^{s-4-(s-8)/3+\varepsilon}&2\nmid s,\end{cases}

(3)

\max_{i=1,2}E_{2,i}(P,\delta)\ll\begin{cases}P^{s-4-(s-10)/3-1/6+\varepsilon}&2\mid s,\\
P^{s-4-(s-9)/3-1/15+\varepsilon}&2\nmid s,\text{ under GLH},\\
P^{s-4-(s-11)/3-1/15+\varepsilon}&2\nmid s.\end{cases}

Combining (
11.2
) and Lemma
11.3
, we establish Lemma
5.3
(
c
) for the term
N_{2}(P,\delta)
defined in (
5.5
).

In the following sections, we shall first prove Lemma
11.3
(
1
) in Section
11.2
. Then we provide some preliminary results in Section
12
and use them to prove Lemma
11.3
(
2
) in Section
13.1
and Lemma
11.3
(
3
) in Section
13.2
for even
s
and Section
13.3
for odd
s
.

11.2.
Contribution to
N_{2,0}(P,\delta)
from
\mathbf{u}={\text{\boldmath$0$}}

We consider
N_{2,0}(P,\delta)
in (
11.2
) from
V<1
, where only the term
\mathbf{u}={\text{\boldmath$0$}}
contributes.
Recall from the comments preceding (
11.3
) that
the condition
V<1
restricts the pairs
(Y,W)
appearing in (
11.1
) to the set
\mathscr{C}_{\delta}^{-}
.

Therefore, the contribution from
V<1
terms on the right side of (
11.1
) can be bounded by

(11.9)

\begin{split}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}KDC\asymp Q^{1/2}\\
D\ll Y\ll Q/K\\
(Y,W)\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}^{-}\end{subarray}}Y^{-s}\sum_{k\asymp K,d\asymp D}\sum_{|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C}\sum_{q\asymp Y}|S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}({\text{\boldmath$0$}})|\\
\times\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\asymp W}|p_{2,\vec{\mathrm{r}},k,q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},{\text{\boldmath$0$}})|\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}.\end{split}

The integral
|I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},{\text{\boldmath$0$}})|
can be estimated by applying Lemma
6.3
together with the trivial bound
(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}\leq 1
. The exponential sum
|S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}({\text{\boldmath$0$}})|
can be estimated using the first inequality in Lemma
7.11
. The sum in (
11.9
) can therefore be bounded by

\begin{split}&\ll WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-(s-1)/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\asymp Y\\
d\asymp D,d\mid q\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\asymp C,k\asymp K\end{subarray}}q^{-s}|S_{q,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{0})|\\
&\ll WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-(s-1)/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\asymp D\\
k\asymp K\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\asymp Y\\
d\mid q\end{subarray}}\sum_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\asymp C}q^{-s}dq^{s/2+1}\gcd\big(\frac{q}{d},\det M_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}\big)^{1/2}\\
&\ll WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-(s-1)/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\asymp D\\
k\asymp K\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\asymp Y\\
d\mid q\end{subarray}}q^{-s}q^{s/2+1}d\sum_{x\mid\frac{q}{d}}x^{1/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\asymp C\\
x\mid\det(M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})\end{subarray}}1\\
&\ll Y^{\varepsilon}WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-(s-1)/2}Y^{-s/2+1}D\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\asymp D\\
k\asymp K\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\asymp Y\\
d\mid q\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\mid\frac{q}{d}\end{subarray}}C(1+\frac{C}{x^{1/2}})x^{1/2},\end{split}

where we used (
9.3
) in the last inequality. It follows that (
11.9
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}KDC\asymp Q^{1/2}\\
D\ll Y\ll Q/K\\
(Y,W)\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}^{-}\end{subarray}}WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-(s-1)/2}DKY^{-s/2+1}Y

\displaystyle\hskip 156.49014pt\times\left(Y^{1/2}D^{-1/2}C+C^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}DC\ll Q^{1/2}\\
D\ll Y\ll Q\\
(Y,W)\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}^{-}\end{subarray}}WQ^{-1}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-(s-1)/2}Y^{-s/2+2}

(11.10)

\displaystyle\hskip 156.49014pt\times\left(Y^{1/2}D^{-1/2}+C\right).

To estimate the maximal value of this expression for
(Y,W)\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}^{-}
, we consider the case
Y\ll Q^{1/2-\delta}
and
Y\gg Q^{1/2-\delta}
separately. We also choose
\delta
sufficiently small (depending on
\varepsilon
and
s
).
If
Y\ll Q^{1/2-\delta}
, then
\mathscr{C}_{\delta}^{-}
implies that
P^{2}W\gg Q^{-\delta}
. As a result, the maximal in (
11.10
) is achieved when
W\asymp Y^{-1}Q^{-1-\delta}
is the smallest (as
s\geq 2
), which hands us the bound (when
\delta
is sufficiently small)

\displaystyle\ll Y^{-1}Q^{-1}Q^{-1}P^{s+\varepsilon}(1+Q^{1/2}/Y)^{-(s-1)/2}Y^{-s/2+2}(Y^{1/2}D^{-1/2}+C)

\displaystyle\ll P^{s+\varepsilon}Y^{1/2}Q^{-s/4-7/4}(Y^{1/2}D^{-1/2}+C).

Under the conditions
Y\ll Q^{1/2-\delta}
and
DC\ll Q^{1/2}
, the maximal is achieved when
Y\asymp Q^{1/2-\delta},C\asymp Q^{1/2}
. Therefore, this contribution to (
11.10
) can be estimated by

\displaystyle\ll P^{s+\varepsilon}Q^{-s/4-1}\ll P^{s-(s+4)/3+\varepsilon}\ll P^{s-4-(s-8)/3+\varepsilon}

when
\delta
is sufficiently small.
If
Y\gg Q^{1/2-\delta}
, then (
11.10
) is maximal when
W\asymp Q^{-3/2}
and
Y\asymp Q^{1/2-\delta}
or
W\asymp Y^{-1}P^{-1}
and
Y\asymp P
, whose contributes to (
11.10
) can be bounded by

\displaystyle Q^{-5/2}Q^{-s/4+1}P^{s+\varepsilon}(Q^{1/4}+Q^{1/2})+P^{-1}P^{-1}Q^{-1}P^{s+\varepsilon}P^{-s/2+2}(P^{1/2}+Q^{1/2})

\displaystyle\ll Q^{-s/4-1}P^{s+\varepsilon}+Q^{-1/2}P^{s/2+\varepsilon}\ll P^{s-4-(s-8)/3+\varepsilon},

for
s\geq 9
when
\delta
is sufficiently small. We have thus established Lemma
11.3
(
1
).

12.
Auxiliary estimates for averages of

|S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}|\times\Sigma
.

Using Lemma
11.2
, it is enough to consider averages of
S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}
and
\Sigma
.
In this section we give upper bounds for these averages using the estimates for exponential sums and integrals from sections
6
and
7
. We provide these upper bounds together with the counting results from Section
9
for preparation of Lemma
11.3
(
3
), which will ultimately suffice to prove Lemma
5.3
(
c
).

12.1.
Estimates for
\Sigma

We will choose an integer parameter
\mathfrak{P}
differently depending on whether
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is a good pair or a bad pair, in order to estimate
\Sigma(r,Y_{1},W,\mathfrak{P};k,d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u})
.

Lemma 12.1

(Type I primes for good
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
and good primes for bad
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
)
.

Under the notation of Lemma
11.2
, we have the following:

(1)

Let
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is good and let
\mathfrak{P}=dD_{F}\det M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
. Then unconditionally we have the following bound

\displaystyle\Sigma(r,Y_{1},W,\mathfrak{P};k,d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle\ll WQ^{-3/2}P^{s+\varepsilon}(1+P^{2}W)^{-(s-1)/2}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}

(12.1)

\displaystyle\quad\times\begin{cases}Y_{1}^{-s/2+3/2}&2\nmid s,\\
Y_{1}^{-s/2+1}&2\mid s,F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\neq 0,\\
Y_{1}^{-s/2+2}&2\mid s,F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0.\end{cases}

If we further assume the generalized Lindelöf hypothesis for Dirichlet L-functions, then for
2\nmid s
and
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\not=\square
, we may obtain

(12.2)

\displaystyle\quad\Sigma(r,Y_{1},W,\mathfrak{P};k,d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle\ll WQ^{-3/2}P^{s+\varepsilon}(1+P^{2}W)^{-(s-1)/2}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}Y_{1}^{-s/2+1}.

(2)

Suppose
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is bad and let
\mathfrak{P}=dD_{F}
. Then
we have

\displaystyle\Sigma(r,Y_{1},W,\mathfrak{P};k,d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle\ll WQ^{-3/2}P^{s+\varepsilon}(1+P^{2}W)^{-(s-1)/2}Y_{1}^{-s/2+3/2+\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2},

if
Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})\not=0
or
((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}\neq 0
,
and otherwise,

\displaystyle\Sigma(r,Y_{1},W,\mathfrak{P};k,d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle\ll WQ^{-3/2}P^{s+\varepsilon}(1+P^{2}W)^{-(s-1)/2}Y_{1}^{-s/2+5/2+\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}.

Proof.

We write
\Sigma(r,Y_{1})=\Sigma(r,Y_{1},W,\mathfrak{P};k,d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u})
for short.

(1) Let
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
be a good pair.
Using Lemma
6.3
with
j=0
, we have

\displaystyle|\Sigma(r,Y_{1})|

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\asymp Y_{1}\\
(q_{1},k\mathfrak{P})=1\end{subarray}}q_{1}^{-s}|S_{q_{1},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\asymp W}|p_{2,dk{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},q_{1}r}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})I_{q_{1}r}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})|d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}

\displaystyle\ll WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-(s-1)/2}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}

(12.3)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\asymp Y_{1}\\
(q_{1},k\mathfrak{P})=1\end{subarray}}q_{1}^{-s}|S_{q_{1},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|.

Using Lemma
7.6
, for
2\mid s
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\asymp Y_{1}\\
(q_{1},k\mathfrak{P})=1\end{subarray}}q_{1}^{-s}|S_{q_{1},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\asymp Y_{1}\\
(q_{1},k\mathfrak{P})=1\end{subarray}}q_{1}^{-s/2}(F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}),q_{1})\ll Y_{1}^{-s/2+1+\mathds{1}_{F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0}+\varepsilon}.

Similarly, for
2\nmid s,F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\not=0
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\asymp Y_{1}\\
(q_{1},k\mathfrak{P})=1\end{subarray}}q_{1}^{-s}|S_{q_{1},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\asymp Y\\
(q_{1},k\mathfrak{P})=1\end{subarray}}Y_{1}^{-s/2+1/2}(F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}),q_{1})^{1/2}\ll Y_{1}^{-s/2+3/2+\varepsilon}.

If
2\nmid s
and
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0
, we see by Lemma
7.6
that

S_{p^{k},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})=0
for
2\nmid k
and

S_{p^{k},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})\ll p^{ks/2+k}
for
2\mid k
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\asymp Y_{1}\\
(q_{1},k\mathfrak{P})=1\end{subarray}}q_{1}^{-s}|S_{q_{1},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\asymp Y_{1}\\
p\mid q_{1}\Rightarrow p^{2}\mid q_{1}\\
(q_{1},k\mathfrak{P})=1\end{subarray}}Y_{1}^{-s/2+1+\varepsilon}\ll P^{\varepsilon}Y_{1}^{-s/2+3/2+\varepsilon}.

This completes the proof of the unconditional bound (
12.1
) for
\Sigma(r,Y_{1})
.

When
s
is odd and
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\not=\square
, we can improve the estimate for
\Sigma(r,Y_{1})
by using cancellations in the sum
\sum_{q_{1}}S_{q_{1},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})
, using Lemma
7.7
. To be precise, we use integration by parts to write

(12.4)

\begin{split}\Sigma(r,Y_{1})={}&\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\asymp W}\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y_{1}\leq q^{\prime}\leq q_{1}\\
(q^{\prime},k\mathfrak{P})=1\end{subarray}}\left(S_{q^{\prime},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})\right)\Big)q_{1}^{-s}p_{2}(q_{1}r,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})I_{q_{1}r}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})\Big|_{Y_{1}}^{2Y_{1}}\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\\
&-\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\asymp W}\int_{Y_{1}\leq q_{1}\leq 2Y_{1}}\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y_{1}\leq q^{\prime}\leq q_{1}\\
(q^{\prime},k\mathfrak{P})=1\end{subarray}}\left(S_{q^{\prime},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})\right)\Big)\\
&\hskip 40.00006pt\times\partial_{q_{1}}\big(q_{1}^{-s}p_{2}(q_{1}r,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})I_{q_{1}r}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})\big)\,dq_{1}d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}.\end{split}

For any
q_{1}\ll Y_{1}
, using Lemma
7.7
, we have

(12.5)

\Big|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y_{1}\leq q^{\prime}\leq q_{1}\\
(q_{1},k\mathfrak{P})=1\end{subarray}}S_{q^{\prime},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})\Big|\ll Y_{1}^{s/2+1+\varepsilon}.

The desired bound for the first term on the right hand side of (
12.4
) follows upon applying Lemma
6.3
to estimate the integral over
{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}
and combining it with (
12.5
).
For the second term in (
12.4
), we change the order of integrals and use (
12.5
) to obtain

(12.6)

\begin{split}\qquad&\int_{Y_{1}\leq q_{1}\leq 2Y_{1}}\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y_{1}\leq q^{\prime}\leq q_{1}\\
(q^{\prime},k\mathfrak{P})=1\end{subarray}}\left(S_{q^{\prime},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})\right)\Big)\\
&\quad\times\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\asymp W}\partial_{q_{1}}\big(q_{1}^{-s}p_{2}(q_{1}r,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})I_{q_{1}r}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})\big)\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\,dq_{1}\\
&\qquad\ll Y_{1}^{\frac{s}{2}+1+\varepsilon}\int_{q_{1}\asymp Y_{1}}\Big|\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\asymp W}\partial_{q_{1}}\big(q_{1}^{-s}p_{2}(q_{1}r,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})I_{q_{1}r}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})\big)\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\Big|\,dq_{1}.\end{split}

From the chain rule with respect to
q_{1}
for a fixed
{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}
, we have

(12.7)

\begin{split}&\quad\partial_{q_{1}}\big(q_{1}^{-s}p_{2}(q_{1}r,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})I_{q_{1}r}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})\big)\\
&=-q_{1}^{-s-1}p_{2}(q_{1}r,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})I_{q_{1}r}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})+q_{1}^{-s}I_{q_{1}r}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})r\partial_{q}p_{2}(q_{1}r,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})\\
&\quad+q_{1}^{-s}p_{2}(q_{1}r,{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})r\partial_{q}I_{q_{1}r}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u}).\end{split}

By substituting (
12.7
) into (
12.6
), we apply Lemma
6.3
to estimate the contribution from the first two terms of (
12.7
), and Lemma
6.4
to estimate the contribution from the final term in (
12.7
). This yields the desired bound for the expression in (
12.4
), under the hypothesis of Lemma
7.7
, allowing us to conclude (
12.2
).

(2)
When
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is a bad pair, Lemma
7.9
hands us

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\asymp Y_{1}\\
(q_{1},dD_{F})=1\end{subarray}}q_{1}^{-s}S_{q_{1},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{q_{1}\asymp Y_{1}}q_{1}^{-s/2-1/2+\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}\gcd(q_{1},Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}))\gcd(q_{1},((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}).

The result follows by summing over
q_{1}
depending on whether
Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})=0
or
((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}=0
, along with an application of (
12.3
) with
\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}=0
.
∎

12.2.
Averages of
|S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}|

Next, we need to consider the
r
-sum appearing in Lemma
11.2
.
An easy bound for the
r
-sum is given in following lemma.

Lemma 12.2

(Trivial Bound)
.

Let
A>0
be some large fixed number.
For any
d\asymp D,1\ll R\ll Q
, we have uniformly for
\mathfrak{P}\ll Q^{A}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r\asymp R\\
r\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\infty}\end{subarray}}|S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|\ll Q^{\varepsilon}D^{1/2}R^{s/2+3/2}

Proof.

The lemma follows from Lemma
7.11
and that the number of
r\asymp R
with
r\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\infty}
is
O(Q^{\varepsilon})
.
∎

12.2.1.
Improved bound for bad
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}

For bad
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
, the contributions from good primes are larger in Lemma
12.1
and our weak bound in Lemma
12.2
is insufficient due the loss of the
d^{1/2}
factor in Lemma
7.11
, and we need to use the improved averaged bound from Lemma
12.3
, making use of Lemma
7.12
for the
d
-sum.

Lemma 12.3
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
.
Suppose that
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is bad. Under the notation of Lemma
11.2
, for
\delta
sufficiently small (depending on
\varepsilon
and
s
) and any choice of
(K,D,C,R,Y,W)\in\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}
, we have

(12.8)

\begin{split}&WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{(1-s)/2}(Y/R)^{-s/2+3/2+\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}\\
&\quad\qquad\times\quad\quad\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{k\asymp K}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})\not=0\\
\text{ or }((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}\not=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r\asymp R\\
r\mid(dD_{F})^{\infty}\end{subarray}}R^{-s}|S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|\\
&\ll P^{s-4-(s-8-\mathds{1}_{2\mid s})/3+\varepsilon}.\end{split}

Proof.

We further split the
d
and
r
-sum into
d=d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}
, where we have
\gcd(d_{1}d_{2},D_{F})=1
, the
d_{i}
are pairwise co-prime,
d_{1}d_{2}
is square-free with
\gcd(d_{1},r/d_{1})=1
,
d_{2}^{2}\mid r
and
d_{3}
consists of numbers whose prime divisors
p
satisfies
p\mid D_{F}
or
p^{2}\mid d_{3}
. We also need to write
r=d_{1}r_{3}
, with
d_{2}^{2}d_{3}\mid r_{3}
and
r_{3}\mid(d_{2}d_{3}D_{F})^{\infty}
.
We will re-order the sums as

\sum_{d_{2}}\sum_{d_{3}}\sum_{r_{3}}\sum_{\mathbf{u}}\sum_{d_{1}}(\cdots),

and split the sums
d_{i}\asymp D_{i},r_{3}\asymp R_{3}
into dyadic ranges.
From Lemma
7.12
, we see that for
\mathbf{u}\not={\text{\boldmath$0$}}

(12.9)

\sum_{d_{1}\asymp D_{1}}|S_{d_{1},d_{1}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|\ll D_{1}^{s/2+3/2}\sum_{d_{1}\asymp D_{1}}\gcd(d_{1},\mathbf{u})^{1/2}\ll D_{1}^{s/2+5/2+\varepsilon},

and from Lemma
7.11
, we have

(12.10)

|S_{r_{3},d_{2}d_{3}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|\ll D_{2}D_{3}R_{3}^{s/2+1}\gcd(r_{3}/d_{2}d_{3},((S^{-1})^{t}\mathbf{u})_{s}))^{1/2}.

Moreover, there are
O(R_{3}^{\varepsilon})
different values of
r_{3}
for fixed
d_{2},d_{3}
and there are
O(D_{2})
numbers of
d_{2}\asymp D_{2}
and
O(D_{3}^{1/2+\varepsilon})
numbers of
d_{3}\asymp D_{3}
for any
\varepsilon>0
. From (
12.9
) and (
12.10
) we have

\displaystyle\sum_{d_{2}}\sum_{d_{3}}\sum_{r_{3}}\sum_{\mathbf{u}}\sum_{d_{1}}|S_{d_{1}r_{1}r_{3},d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}D_{1}^{s/2+5/2}D_{2}D_{3}R_{3}^{s/2+1}\sum_{d_{2},d_{3}}\sum_{r_{3}}\sum_{x_{1}\mid\frac{r_{3}}{d_{2}d_{3}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\neq|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
x_{1}\mid((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}\end{subarray}}x_{1}^{1/2}.

After an application of (
9.2
) to estimate the
\mathbf{u}
sum, this is

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}D_{1}^{s/2+5/2}D_{2}D_{3}R_{3}^{s/2+1}\sum_{d_{2},d_{3}}\sum_{r_{3}}\sum_{x_{1}\mid r_{3}/d_{2}d_{3}}(x_{1}^{1/2}V^{s-1}+V^{s})

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}D_{1}^{s/2+5/2}D_{2}^{2}D_{3}^{3/2}R_{3}^{s/2+1}V^{s-1}(R_{3}^{1/2}D_{2}^{-1/2}D_{3}^{-1/2}+V)

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}R^{s/2+3/2}D_{1}D_{2}^{2}D_{3}^{3/2}V^{s-1}(D_{2}^{-1/2}D_{3}^{-1/2}+VR_{3}^{-1/2}).

Substituting back in (
12.8
), the total contribution is

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}Y^{-s/2+3/2+\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}R^{-\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{(1-s)/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\ \ \times D_{1}D_{2}^{2}D_{3}^{3/2}KV^{s-1}(D_{2}^{-1/2}D_{3}^{-1/2}+VR_{3}^{-1/2})

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}Y^{-s/2+3/2+\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}R^{-\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}WQ^{-1}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{(1-s)/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\ \ \times D_{2}D_{3}^{1/2}V^{s-1}(D_{2}^{-1/2}D_{3}^{-1/2}+VR_{3}^{-1/2}),

since
D_{1}D_{2}D_{3}K\asymp Q^{1/2}
.
Note that as
d_{2}^{2}d_{3}\mid r_{3}
, and also
d_{2}^{2}\mid r
and
d_{2}\mid d
, this contribution is

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}D_{2}\geq 1\\
D_{2}\leq R^{1/2}\\
D_{2}\leq D\end{subarray}}Y^{-s/2+3/2+\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}R^{-\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}WQ^{-1}P^{s}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\ \ \times(1+P^{2}W)^{(1-s)/2}V^{s-1}(D_{2}^{1/2}+V).

When
2\nmid s
, the contribution is maximized when
K=D_{1}=D_{3}=1
and
D_{2}\asymp Q^{1/2}
, and it is bounded by

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}Y^{-s/2+3/2}WQ^{-1}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{(1-s)/2}V^{s-1}(Q^{1/4}+V).

The maximum value is reached when
W=Q^{-1/2+\delta}Y^{-1}
, and therefore
V=Q^{1/4+\varepsilon+\delta}
. Therefore, when
\delta
is sufficiently small (depending on
\varepsilon
and
s
) this contribution is

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}Y^{-s/2+1/2}Q^{-3/2}P^{s}(Q/Y)^{(1-s)/2}Q^{s/4}

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}P^{s}Q^{-s/4-1}\ll P^{s-4-(s-8)/3+\varepsilon},

which completes the case for odd
s
. When
2\mid s
, our contribution is

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{D_{2}}Y^{-s/2+2}R^{-1/2}WQ^{-1}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{(1-s)/2}V^{s-1}(D_{2}^{1/2}+V)

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}Y^{-s/2+2}R^{-1/2}WQ^{-1+1/4}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{(1-s)/2}V^{s-1},

upon bounding
D_{2}^{1/2}+V\ll Q^{1/4+\varepsilon+\delta}
and using that
\delta
is sufficiently small. We next note that
d\mid r_{3}
and
Y\leq Q/K
therefore
Y/R\leq Q/(KD)\leq Q^{1/2}
. Upon further substituting
V=YP^{-1+\varepsilon}(1+P^{2}W)
, the expression is maximum when
W=Y^{-1}Q^{-1/2+\delta}
and therefore
V\asymp Q^{1/4+\varepsilon+\delta}
.
By choosing
\delta
sufficiently small in terms of
\varepsilon
and
s
, we see that the above is

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}Y^{-s/2+3/2}WQ^{-1/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{(1-s)/2}V^{s-1},

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}Y^{-s/2+1/2}Q^{-1}P^{s}(Q/Y)^{(1-s)/2}Q^{s/4-1/4}

\displaystyle\ll P^{s+\varepsilon}Q^{-s/4-3/4}\ll P^{s-4-(s-9)/3+\varepsilon}

which completes the proof for even
s
.
∎

12.2.2.
Improved bound for good
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}

For good
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
, Lemma
12.2
turns out to be insufficient to compensate for our weaker bounds on exponential sums modulo powers of good primes of type II, as well as powers of bad primes. To address this, we combine Lemma
7.8
and Lemma
7.11
with Lemma
9.4
to obtain the following improved bound, which takes advantage of the average over
\mathbf{u}
.

Lemma 12.4
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. Under the notation of Lemma
11.2
, for
\delta
sufficiently small (depending on
\varepsilon
and
s
) any
(K,D,C,R,Y,W)\in\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}
, we have

\begin{split}&Y^{-s/2+1}R^{-s/2-1}WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-(s-1)/2}\\
&\qquad\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\asymp D\\
k\asymp K\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\\
\mathrm{good,primitive}\\
|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r\asymp R\\
r\mid(dD_{F}\det M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{\infty}\end{subarray}}|S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}\\
&\ll P^{s-4-(s-9)/3-1/15+\varepsilon}.\end{split}

Proof.

For any fixed
k,d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u}
, we write
r=r_{2}r_{3}
where
r_{2}\mid\det(M_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}})^{\infty}
,
(r_{2},dD_{F})=1
and
r_{2}
is free of fifth powers, while
r_{3}
consists of the remaining factors, i.e.
r_{3}
consists of numbers whose prime factors divide
dD_{F}
and all
5
-full numbers. Note that the condition
d\mid r
implies
d\mid r_{3}
.
We further split the
r
-sum into
O(R^{\varepsilon})
sums over
r_{2},r_{3}
with with
r_{i}\asymp R_{i}
with
R_{2}R_{3}\asymp R
, so that it is enough to bound

\mathscr{S}=Y^{-s/2+1}WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-(s-1)/2}\\
\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\asymp D\\
k\asymp K\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
\text{primitive}\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\text{ good}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r_{3}\asymp R_{3}\\
r_{3}\mid(dD_{F}\det M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{\infty}\end{subarray}}\\
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r\asymp R\\
r_{2}\mid(\det M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{\infty}\\
(r_{2},r_{3}dD_{F})=1\\
r_{2}\text{ fifth power free}\end{subarray}}(R_{2}R_{3})^{-s/2-1}|S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}.

By Lemma
7.1
, we write

S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})=S_{r_{2},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})S_{r_{3},d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})
and using
Lemma
7.8
and Lemma
7.11
we obtain

(12.11)

\displaystyle|S_{r_{2},{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|

\displaystyle\ll r_{2}^{1+s/2}\gcd(r_{2},((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s},Q^{*}_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}))^{1/2}

(12.12)

\displaystyle|S_{r_{3},d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|

\displaystyle\ll dr_{3}^{s/2+1}\gcd(r_{3}/d,((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s},\det(M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}))^{1/2}.

We arrange the order of summation as

\sum_{k}\sum_{d}\sum_{r_{3}}\sum_{\text{${\vec{\mathrm{c}}}$}}\sum_{r_{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{u}}(\cdots).

Combining our bounds in (
12.11
) and (
12.12
), we see that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r_{3}\\
r_{3}\asymp R_{3}\end{subarray}}\sum_{|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C}\sum_{r_{2}\asymp R_{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{u}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}\\
|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\end{subarray}}(R_{2}R_{3})^{-s/2-1}|S_{r_{2}r_{3},d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}

has an upper bound

\displaystyle\ll D\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{3}\asymp R_{3}\\
d\mid r_{3}\end{subarray}}\sum_{|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}\sum_{r_{2}\asymp R_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}\\
|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\end{subarray}}\gcd(r_{2},((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s},Q^{*}_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}))^{1/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 156.49014pt\times\gcd(r_{3}/d,((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s},\det(M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}))^{1/2},

and hence

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r_{3}\\
r_{3}\asymp R_{3}\end{subarray}}\sum_{|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C}\sum_{r_{2}\asymp R_{2}}\sum_{\mathbf{u}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}\\
|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\end{subarray}}(R_{2}R_{3})^{-s/2-1}|S_{r_{2}r_{3},d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\ll D\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r_{3}\asymp R_{3}\\
x_{1}\mid\frac{r_{3}}{d}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
x_{1}\mid\det(M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})\end{subarray}}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{2}\asymp R_{2}\\
x_{2}\mid r_{2}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
x_{1}x_{2}\mid((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}\\
x_{2}\mid Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})\end{subarray}}(x_{1}x_{2})^{1/2}.

(12.13)

We apply (
9.2
) with
q=x_{1}x_{2},d=x_{2}
to the inner sum of (
12.13
),
along with the observation that
\prod_{p\mid x_{2}}p\gg x_{2}^{1/4}
, since
x_{2}
is free of fifth powers, to obtain

\begin{split}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
x_{1}x_{2}\mid((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}\\
x_{2}\mid Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})\end{subarray}}1&\ll V^{s-2}\min\Big(V+\frac{V^{2}}{x_{1}x_{2}},(1+\frac{V}{x_{2}^{1/4}})^{2}\Big)\\
&\ll V^{s-2}+\frac{V^{s}}{(x_{1}x_{2})^{1/2}}+\min\Big(V^{s-1},\frac{V^{s}}{x_{2}^{1/2}}\Big).\end{split}

We now consider the cases
R_{2}\leq V^{2}
and
R_{2}\geq V^{2}
separately. If
R_{2}\leq V^{2}
, we apply (
9.3
) with
N=P^{2}W
to estimate the sum over
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
, and obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{3}\asymp R_{3}\\
x_{1}\mid\frac{r_{3}}{d}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
x_{1}\mid\det(M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})\end{subarray}}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{2}\asymp R_{2}\\
x_{2}\mid r_{2}\end{subarray}}(x_{1}x_{2})^{1/2}(V^{s}/(x_{1}x_{2})^{1/2}+V^{s-1})

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{3}\asymp R_{3}\\
x_{1}\mid\frac{r_{3}}{d}\end{subarray}}C(1+C/(x_{1}(1+P^{2}W))^{1/2})(V^{s}+x_{1}^{1/2}R_{2}^{1/2}V^{s-1}),

which is turn has the upper bound

\ll P^{\varepsilon}\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{3}\asymp R_{3}\\
x_{1}\mid\frac{r_{3}}{d}\end{subarray}}C^{2}(V^{s}+R_{2}^{1/2}V^{s-1})(1+P^{2}W)^{-1/2}\\
\hskip 56.9055pt+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{3}\asymp R_{3}\\
x_{1}\mid\frac{r_{3}}{d}\end{subarray}}C(V^{s}+x_{1}^{1/2}R_{2}^{1/2}V^{s-1})\Big),

which is

(12.14)

\ll P^{\varepsilon}\Big((R_{3}/D)^{1/5}C^{2}V^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-1/2}\\
+(R_{3}/D)^{1/5}C(V^{s}+(R_{3}R_{2}/D)^{1/2}V^{s-1})\Big).

If
R_{2}\geq V^{2}
, then the contribution from divisors
x_{2}\leq V^{2}
can again be bound analogously as before. For the contribution from
x_{2}\geq V^{2}
, we apply (
9.3
) with
N=P^{2}W
to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{3}\asymp R_{3}\\
x_{1}\mid\frac{r_{3}}{d}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
x_{1}\mid\det(M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})\end{subarray}}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{2}\asymp R_{2}\\
x_{2}\mid r_{2}\\
x_{2}\gg V^{2}\end{subarray}}((x_{1}x_{2})^{1/2}V^{s-2}+x_{1}^{1/2}V^{s})

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{3}\asymp R_{3}\\
x_{1}\mid\frac{r_{3}}{d}\end{subarray}}C(1+C/(x_{1}(1+P^{2}W))^{1/2})((x_{1}R_{2})^{1/2}V^{s-2}+x_{1}^{1/2}V^{s}),

which is

\ll P^{\varepsilon}(R_{3}/D)^{1/5}\Big(C^{2}(V^{s}+R_{2}^{1/2}V^{s-2})(1+P^{2}W)^{-1/2}\\
+C((R_{3}/D)^{1/2}V^{s}+(R_{3}R_{2}/D)^{1/2}V^{s-2})\Big),

which is finally

(12.15)

\ll P^{\varepsilon}(R_{3}/D)^{1/5}\Big(C^{2}(V^{s}+R_{2}^{1/2}V^{s-2})(1+P^{2}W)^{-1/2}\\
+C(R_{3}R_{2}/D)^{1/2}V^{s-1}\Big).

In the last inequality, we have used

(R_{3}/D)^{1/2}V^{s}\ll(R_{3}/D)^{1/2}R_{2}^{1/2}V^{s-1},

since
R_{2}\geq V^{2}
. Combining (
12.14
) and (
12.15
) together, (
12.13
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}D(R_{3}/D)^{1/5}\Big(C^{2}(V^{s}+(R_{2}R_{3}/D)^{1/2}V^{s-2})(1+P^{2}W)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\ \ +C(V^{s}+(R_{3}R_{2}/D)^{1/2}V^{s-1})\Big).

After summing over
d,k
, and using the fact that
DKC\asymp Q^{1/2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle DK(R_{3}/D)^{1/5}\Big(C^{2}(V^{s}+(R_{2}R_{3}/D)^{1/2}V^{s-2})(1+P^{2}W)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+C(V^{s}+(R_{3}R_{2}/D)^{1/2}V^{s-1})\Big)

\displaystyle\ll Q^{1/2}\Big(C(V^{s}(R_{3}/D)^{1/5}+CR_{2}^{1/2}(R_{3}/D)^{7/10}V^{s-2})(1+P^{2}W)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+V^{s}(R_{3}/D)^{1/5}+R_{2}^{1/2}(R_{3}/D)^{7/10}V^{s-1}\Big),

and this is

\displaystyle\ll Q^{1/2}\Big((Q^{1/2}R^{1/5}V^{s}+Q^{1/2}R^{7/10}V^{s-2})(1+P^{2}W)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+R^{1/5}V^{s}+R^{7/10}V^{s-1}\Big).

By taking
V=YP^{-1}(1+P^{2}W)P^{\varepsilon}
, we see that the sum
\mathscr{S}
we need to bound is

\displaystyle\ll Y^{-s/2+1}WQ^{-1}P^{s+\varepsilon}(1+P^{2}W)^{-s/2}\Big(Q^{1/2}R^{1/5}V^{s}+Q^{1/2}R^{7/10}V^{s-2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+R^{1/5}V^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{1/2}+R^{7/10}V^{s-1}(1+P^{2}W)^{1/2}\Big)

\displaystyle\ll Y^{s/2+1}WQ^{-1}(1+P^{2}W)^{s/2}P^{\varepsilon}\Big(Q^{1/2}R^{1/5}+Q^{1/2}R^{7/10}Y^{-2}P^{2}(1+P^{2}W)^{-2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+R^{1/5}(1+P^{2}W)^{1/2}+R^{7/10}Y^{-1}P(1+P^{2}W)^{-1/2}\Big)

\displaystyle\ll Y^{s/2+1}WQ^{-1}(1+P^{2}W)^{s/2}P^{\varepsilon}\Big(Q^{1/2}R^{1/5}+Q^{1/2}R^{7/10}Y^{-2}P^{2}(1+P^{2}W)^{-2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+R^{7/10}Y^{-1}P(1+P^{2}W)^{-1/2}\Big),

using the fact that
(1+P^{2}W)\ll P^{\varepsilon}(Q/Y)\ll Q
since
(Y,W)\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}
and
\delta
is sufficiently small.
As the exponents of
W
and
R
in the above expression are positive, the maximum over
\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}
is attained when
W\asymp Y^{-1}Q^{-1/2}P^{\delta}
which implies
P^{2}W\asymp(Q/Y)P^{\delta}
and
R=Y
. In that maximal case, and when
\delta
is sufficiently small, the upper bound above becomes

\displaystyle\ll Y^{s/2}Q^{-3/2}(Q/Y)^{s/2}P^{\varepsilon}\Big(Q^{7/10}+Q^{-3/2}Y^{7/10}P^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 156.49014pt+Y^{7/10}Y^{-1}P(Q/Y)^{-1/2}\Big);

by noting that the maximum of the latter expression is attained when
Y=Q
, because the resulting exponents of
Y
are positive. The above is

\displaystyle\ll P^{s+\varepsilon}Q^{-s/4-3/2}\Big(Q^{7/10}+Q^{7/10-1/4}\Big)

\displaystyle\ll P^{s+\varepsilon}Q^{-s/4-4/5}

\displaystyle\ll P^{s-(s/3+16/15)+\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\ll P^{s-4-(s-9)/3-1/15+\varepsilon},

by using the relation
Q=P^{4/3}
.
∎

13.
Minor arcs contributions:
N_{2,1}(P,\delta)
and
N_{2,2}(P,\delta)

In this section, we prove Lemma
11.3
(
2
) and Lemma
11.3
(
3
) by considering the contributions from bad pairs of
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
in Section
13.1
, the contributions from good pairs of
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
when
s
is even in Section
13.2
and when
s
is odd in Section
13.3
.

13.1.
N_{2,1}(P,\delta)
contribution from bad pairs
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}

In this section we prove Lemma
11.3
(
2
) for contributions from bad pairs
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
.
In this case, we know
|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\ll 1
. We henceforth fix a bad pair
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
and estimate the minor arc contribution from this pair. Note that when
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is bad, we have
\lambda_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}=0
.
Recall the terms
E_{1,1}(P,\delta)
and
E_{1,2}(P,\delta)
from (
11.6
).

13.1.1.
Case:
Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})\not=0
or
((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}\not=0

Using Lemma
12.1
(
2
) which requires
Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})\not=0
or
((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}\not=0
, we see that

\begin{split}&E_{1,1}(P,\delta)\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta},C\ll 1}\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{k\asymp K}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})\not=0\\
\text{ or }((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}\not=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r\asymp R\\
r\mid(dD_{F})^{\infty}\end{subarray}}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad r^{-s}|S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})\Sigma(r,Y/R,W,\mathfrak{P};k,d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u})|\\
&\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta},C\ll 1}\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{k\asymp K}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})\not=0\\
\text{ or }((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}\not=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r\asymp R\\
r\mid(dD_{F})^{\infty}\end{subarray}}R^{-s}|S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|\\
&\quad\quad\times WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{(1-s)/2}(Y/R)^{-s/2+3/2+\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}.\end{split}

Using Lemma
12.3
,
when
\delta
is sufficiently small (depending on
\varepsilon
and
s
) this contribution is
