\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta},C\ll 1}P^{s}Q^{-s/4-1}\ll P^{s-4-(s-8-\mathds{1}_{2\mid s})/3+\varepsilon},

which suffices for
s\geq 9
.

13.1.2.
Case:
Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})=((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}=0

Using Lemma
12.1
(
2
), we see that

\displaystyle E_{1,2}(P,\delta)\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta},C\ll 1}\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{k\asymp K}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})=((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r\asymp R\\
r\mid(dD_{F})^{\infty}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\ \ r^{-s}|S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})\Sigma(r,Y/R,W,\mathfrak{P};k,d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u})|

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta},C\ll 1}\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{k\asymp K}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})=((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r\asymp R\\
r\mid(dD_{F})^{\infty}\end{subarray}}R^{-s}|S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{(1-s)/2}(Y/R)^{-s/2+5/2+\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}.

Using Lemma
12.2
for the
r
-sum, we see that

\displaystyle E_{1,2}(P,\delta)

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta},C\ll 1}Y^{-s/2+5/2+\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{(1-s)/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\ \ \times D^{3/2}KR^{-1-\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})=((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}=0\end{subarray}}1.

To estimate the sum over
\mathbf{u}
, we note that the conditions

Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})=((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}=0

defines a smooth variety of degree 2 and projective dimension
s-3
, as
Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}
is nonsingular. As a result, using Lemma
9.1
,

\#\{|\mathbf{u}|\leq V:Q_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})=((S^{-1})^{T}\mathbf{u})_{s}=0\}\ll V^{s-3+\varepsilon}.

Thus

\displaystyle E_{1,2}(P,\delta)

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta},C\ll 1}Y^{-s/2+5/2+\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\ \ \times(1+P^{2}W)^{(1-s)/2}D^{3/2}KR^{-1-\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}V^{s-3}.

With
V=\frac{Y}{P}(1+P^{2}W)P^{\varepsilon}
, the maximal is achieved at
W=Y^{-1}Q^{-1/2+\delta}
, which finally gives when
\delta
is sufficiently small

\displaystyle E_{1,2}(P,\delta)

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta},C\ll 1}Y^{-s/2+3/2+\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}Q^{-3/2}P^{s}(Q/Y)^{(1-s)/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\ \ \times D^{1/2}R^{-1-\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}Q^{s/4-3/4}

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta},C\ll 1}Q^{-s/4-3/4+\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}D^{1/2}K^{-1-\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}R^{-1-\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/2}P^{s}

\displaystyle\ll Q^{-s/4-1+\mathds{1}_{2\mid s}/4}P^{s+\varepsilon}\ll P^{s-4-(s-8-\mathds{1}_{2\mid s})/3+\varepsilon},

using
Y\ll Q/K
,
D\ll Q^{1/2}
and
RK\gg DK\asymp Q^{1/2}
.
This is admissible as soon as
s\geq 9
when
\delta
is sufficiently small in terms of
\varepsilon
and
s
.

13.2.
N_{2,2}(P,\delta)
contribution from good pairs
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
for even
s

In this section we prove Lemma
11.3
(
3
) when
s
is even. Recall the sums
E_{2,1}(P,\delta)
and
E_{2,2}(P,\delta)
as corresponding contributions coming from an application of (
11.7
).

13.2.1.
The case
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\not=0

Using Lemma
12.1
(
1
), we see that
unconditionally
for even
s
and
\delta
sufficiently small (depending on
\varepsilon
and
s
)

\displaystyle E_{2,1}(P,\delta)\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{k\asymp K}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\text{ good}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
F_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\not=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r\asymp R\\
r\mid(2dD_{F}\det M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{\infty}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102ptr^{-s}|S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})\Sigma(r,Y/R,W,\mathfrak{P};k,d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u})|

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{k\asymp K}\sum_{|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C}\,\,\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
F_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\not=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r\asymp R\\
r\mid(2dD_{F}\det M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{\infty}\end{subarray}}R^{-s}|S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|

\displaystyle\times WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{(1-s)/2}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}(Y/R)^{-s/2+1},

which gives

(13.1)

\displaystyle E_{2,1}(P,\delta)\ll P^{s-4-(s-9)/3-1/15+\varepsilon}

by
using Lemma
12.4
.

13.2.2.
The case
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0

In this case, Lemma
12.1
gives

\displaystyle E_{2,2}(P,\delta)\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{k\asymp K}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\text{ good}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
F_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r\asymp R\\
r\mid(2dD_{F}\det M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{\infty}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102ptr^{-s}|S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})\Sigma(r,Y/R,W,\mathfrak{P};k,d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u})|

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{k\asymp K}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\text{ good}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
F_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r\asymp R\\
r\mid(2dD_{F}\det M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{\infty}\end{subarray}}R^{-s}|S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|

\displaystyle\times WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{(1-s)/2}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}(Y/R)^{-s/2+2}.

Using Lemma
12.2
for the
r
-sum we see that

\displaystyle E_{2,2}(P,\delta)\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{k\asymp K}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\text{ good}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
F_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0\end{subarray}}Y^{-s/2+2}WQ^{-3/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{(1-s)/2}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}D^{1/2}R^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}Y^{-s/2+2}R^{-1/2}D^{3/2}KWQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{(1-s)/2}

(13.2)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\text{ good}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
F_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0\end{subarray}}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}})^{-1/2}.

Now we focus on the
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u}
sum. For a fixed good
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
,
F^{*}_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathbf{u})
is an irreducible quadratic form in
\mathbf{u}
and therefore by
[
15
, Theorem 2]
we have

(13.3)

\#\{|\mathbf{u}|\leq V:F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0\}\ll V^{s-2+\varepsilon}.

However, this saving in the
V
variable together with (
9.3
) will not be enough. Therefore, we further split the sum over
\mathbf{u}
into terms for which
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\neq 0
and
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0
.

For fixed
\mathbf{u}
such that
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\neq 0
, we note that the polynomial
F^{*}_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathbf{u})
must be a non-zero polynomial of degree
s-1
in the
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
variable.
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})
is the discriminant of the polynomial
F^{*}_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathbf{u})
, seen as a polynomial in
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
. Therefore,
F^{*}_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathbf{u})
has at most
s-1
primitive roots. As a result, we reach an alternate bound:

(13.4)

\#\{|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\text{ good},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V:{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\neq 0,F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0\}\ll V^{s+\varepsilon}.

Combining (
13.3
) with (
9.3
) and comparing this bound with (
13.4
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
\textrm{ good primitive}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0,{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\neq 0\end{subarray}}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\ll V^{s-2+\varepsilon}C+V^{s-2+\varepsilon}\min\{C^{2}(1+P^{2}W)^{-1/2},V^{2}\}

\displaystyle\ll V^{s-2+\varepsilon}C+V^{s-3/2+\varepsilon}C^{3/2}(1+P^{2}W)^{-3/8}

(13.5)

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}C\Big(V^{s-2}+V^{s-3/2}C^{1/2}(1+P^{2}W)^{-3/8}\Big).

Now we consider the case
\mathscr{F}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0
.
If
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
is good and
s\geq 8
, then Lemma
9.2
shows that the variety
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0
has projective dimension
s-3
and no components of degree 1 or 3. Hence, applying Lemma
9.1
to each component of
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0
, we find

\displaystyle\{|\mathbf{u}|\leq V:{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=F^{*}_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathbf{u})=0)\}\ll V^{s-3+\varepsilon}.

Therefore,

(13.6)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
\textrm{ good primitive}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0,{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0\end{subarray}}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}\\
\ll V^{s-3+\varepsilon}C(1+C(1+P^{2}W)^{-1/2}).

Combining (
13.5
) and (
13.6
) we see that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
\textrm{ good primitive}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0\end{subarray}}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}C\Big(V^{s-3/2}C^{1/2}(1+P^{2}W)^{-3/8}+V^{s-2}+V^{s-3}C(1+P^{2}W)^{-1/2}\Big).

Substituting these bounds in (
13.2
), we obtain

\displaystyle E_{2,2}(P,\delta)\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}Y^{-s/2+2}R^{-1/2}WD^{3/2}KCQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{(1-s)/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\Big(V^{s-3/2}C^{1/2}(1+P^{2}W)^{-3/8}+V^{s-2}+V^{s-3}C(1+P^{2}W)^{-1/2}\Big).

Recall that
DKC\asymp Q^{1/2},V=YP^{-1}(1+P^{2}W)P^{\varepsilon}
and
W\ll Y^{-1}Q^{-1/2}P^{\delta}
. We see that the maximum is reached when
W\asymp Y^{-1}Q^{-1/2}P^{\delta},R\asymp 1
and hence
1+P^{2}W\asymp P^{\delta}Q/Y,V\asymp P^{1/3+\varepsilon+\delta}
. Thus, for
\delta
is sufficiently small (depending on
\varepsilon
and
s
), we have

\displaystyle E_{2,2}(P,\delta)

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}Y^{-s/2+1}D^{1/2}Q^{-3/2}P^{s}(Q/Y)^{(1-s)/2}C^{1/2}

\displaystyle\times\Big(P^{s/3-1/2}(Q/Y)^{-3/8}+P^{s/3-2/3}+P^{s/3-1}C^{1/2}(Q/Y)^{-1/2}\Big).

Further, the maximum is attained when
Y\asymp Q,R\asymp 1

and upon further using
KDC\asymp Q^{1/2}
and
\delta
sufficiently small, we obtain

\displaystyle E_{2,2}(P,\delta)

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}Q^{-s/2+1+1/4}Q^{-3/2}P^{s}\Big(P^{s/3-1/2}+P^{s/3-2/3}+P^{s/3-1}Q^{1/4}\Big)

\displaystyle\ll P^{s+\varepsilon}Q^{-s/2-1/4}\Big(P^{s/3-1/2}+P^{s/3-2/3}\Big)

(13.7)

\displaystyle\qquad\ll P^{s-s/3-2/3-1/6+\varepsilon}\ll P^{s-4-(s-10)/3-1/6+\varepsilon}.

Combing (
13.1
) and (
13.7
), we obtain Lemma
11.3
(
3
) when
s
is even.

13.3.
N_{2,2}(P,\delta)
from good pairs
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
for odd
s

In this section, we prove Lemma
11.3
(
3
) when
s
is odd.
Recall that the contributions
E_{2,1}(P,\delta)
and
E_{2,2}(P,\delta)
arise from the splitting (
11.8
).
We will first assume the generalized Lindelöf hypothesis (GLH) as in Lemma
7.7
for
E_{2,1}(P,\delta)
and the proof for the unconditional case follows similarly by simple modifications which we shall describe at the end. The estimation for
E_{2,2}(P,\delta)
is unconditional.

13.3.1.
Case:
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\neq\square

When
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\neq\square
, we can use Lemma
12.1
to see that for
\delta
sufficiently small and under GLH

\displaystyle E_{2,1}(P,\delta)\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{k\asymp K}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\text{ good}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
F_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\not=\square\end{subarray}}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r\asymp R\\
r\mid(2dD_{F}\det M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{\infty}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad r^{-s}|S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})\Sigma(r,Y/R,W,\mathfrak{P};k,d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u})|

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}Y^{-s/2+1}R^{-s/2-1}WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-(s-1)/2}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{k\asymp K}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\text{ good}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
F_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\not=\square\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r\asymp R\\
r\mid(2dD_{F}\det M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{\infty}\end{subarray}}|S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})|^{-1/2},

which gives

(13.8)

E_{2,1}(P,\delta)\ll P^{s-4-(s-9)/3-1/15+\varepsilon}

by
using Lemma
12.4
. Unconditionally, we see that Lemma
12.1
gives an extra factor of
Y_{1}^{1/2}\ll Q^{1/2}=P^{2/3}
compared to the bound (
13.8
) under GLH, yielding

(13.9)

E_{2,1}(P,\delta)\ll P^{s-4-(s-11)/3-1/15+\varepsilon}

unconditionally.

13.3.2.
Case:
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=\square

We now consider the sum over
\mathbf{u}
for which
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=\square
. In this case, Lemma
12.1
gives

\displaystyle E_{2,2}(P,\delta)\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{k\asymp K}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\text{ good}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
F_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=\square\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r\asymp R\\
r\mid(2dD_{F}\det M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{\infty}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad r^{-s}|S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})\Sigma(r,Y/R,W,\mathfrak{P};k,d,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u})|

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{k\asymp K}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\text{ good}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
F_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=\square\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mid r\asymp R\\
r\mid(2dD_{F}\det M_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{\infty}\end{subarray}}R^{-s}|S_{r,d{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}(\mathbf{u})|

\displaystyle\times WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-(s-1)/2}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}(Y/R)^{-s/2+3/2}.

Using Lemma
12.2
for the
r
-sum we obtain

\displaystyle E_{2,2}(P,\delta)\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}\sum_{d\asymp D}\sum_{k\asymp K}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\text{ good}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
F_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=\square\end{subarray}}Y^{-s/2+3/2}WQ^{-3/2}P^{s}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times(1+P^{2}W)^{(1-s)/2}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}D^{1/2}

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}Y^{-s/2+3/2}D^{3/2}KWQ^{-3/2}P^{s}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times(1+P^{2}W)^{-(s-1)/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C\\
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\text{ good}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
F_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=\square\end{subarray}}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}})^{-1/2}.

We now estimate the sum over primitive good
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
and
\mathbf{u}
such that
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=\square
by considering whether
\mathscr{F}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0
or not. If
\mathscr{F}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\not=0
, we can bound the
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}},\mathbf{u}
sum in two ways. First, we fix such a
\mathbf{u}
and estimate the sum over
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
using
[
5
, Theorem 5]
. Here, for a fixed
\mathbf{u}
, we see
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=z^{2}
as a polynomial of degree
s-1
in the
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
variable. Moreover, when
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\neq 0
,
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})
is square-free and therefore this is an irreducible polynomial in
z
. Moreover,
F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=(z^{(s-1)/2})^{2}
would be a homogeneous polynomial in
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
and
z
. Therefore, the hypothesis of
[
5
, Theorem 5]
is indeed satisfied and as a result we obtain:

(13.10)

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C,|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\neq 0,F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=\square\end{subarray}}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}})^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\#\{|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\textrm{ primitive and good, }F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=\square\}\ll P^{\varepsilon}V^{s}C.

In a different approach, we first fix a good
{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}
and estimate the sum over
\mathbf{u}
using a slight generalization of
[
15
, Theorem 2]
obtained in
[
37
, Lemma 3.7]
:

(13.11)

\#\{|\mathbf{u}|\leq V:F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=z^{2}:|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\}\ll V^{s-1+\varepsilon}.

Combining it with the estimate for
\sum_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}})^{-1/2}
from (
9.3
) to obtain the second bound:

(13.12)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\text{ good},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\neq 0,F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=\square\end{subarray}}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}})^{-1/2}\ll P^{\varepsilon}\Big(V^{s-1}C+V^{s-1}C^{2}(1+P^{2}W)^{-1/2}\Big).

Combining (
13.10
) and (
13.12
) we see that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\text{ good},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})\neq 0,F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=\square\end{subarray}}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}})^{-1/2}

(13.13)

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}V^{s-1}(1+P^{2}W)^{-1/2}C\min\{V(1+P^{2}W)^{1/2},C\}.

If
\mathscr{F}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0
, then we use Lemma
9.1
to estimate

\displaystyle\#\{|\mathbf{u}|\leq V:{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0,F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=\square\}

\displaystyle\ll\#\{|\mathbf{u}|\leq V:{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0\}

\displaystyle\ll V^{s-2+\varepsilon},

so that

(13.14)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}|\asymp C,{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}\text{ good},|\mathbf{u}|\leq V\\
{\mathscr{F}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=0,F_{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}^{*}(\mathbf{u})=\square\end{subarray}}(1+P^{2}W\lambda_{{\vec{\mathrm{c}}}})^{-1/2}\ll P^{\varepsilon}\Big(V^{s-2}C+V^{s-2}C^{2}(1+P^{2}W)^{-1/2}\Big).

Combining (
13.13
) and (
13.14
), we obtain

\displaystyle E_{2,2}(P,\delta)

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}Y^{-s/2+3/2}D^{3/2}KWQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-s/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times V^{s-1}C\min\{V(1+P^{2}W)^{1/2},C\}

\displaystyle\quad+\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}Y^{-s/2+3/2}D^{3/2}KWQ^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-s/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times V^{s-2}C((1+P^{2}W)^{1/2}+C)

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}Y^{-s/2+3/2}D^{1/2}WQ^{-1}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-s/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times C^{1/2}(1+P^{2}W)^{1/4}V^{s-1/2}

\displaystyle\quad+\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}Y^{-s/2+3/2}D^{1/2}WQ^{-1}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-s/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times((1+P^{2}W)^{1/2}+C)V^{s-2}

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}Y^{-s/2+3/2}D^{1/2}WQ^{-1}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-s/2}

(13.15)

\displaystyle\times(C^{1/2}(1+P^{2}W)^{1/4}+((1+P^{2}W)^{1/2}+C)V^{-3/2})V^{s-1/2}.

Comparing the powers of
D,C
and
K
in the above expression, since
KDC\asymp Q^{1/2}
, the above expression is maximized when
C\asymp Q^{1/2},DK\asymp 1
and this the above is

\begin{split}&\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}Y^{-s/2+3/2}WQ^{-1}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-s/2}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times(Q^{1/4}(1+P^{2}W)^{1/4}+((1+P^{2}W)^{1/2}+Q^{1/2})V^{-3/2})V^{s-1/2}.\end{split}

Recalling that
V=YP^{-1}(1+P^{2}W)P^{\varepsilon}
and
W\ll Y^{-1}Q^{-1/2}P^{\delta}
, we see that (
13.15
) again takes maximum when
W\asymp Y^{-1}Q^{-1/2}P^{\delta}
, which means
1+P^{2}W\asymp(Q/Y)P^{\delta}
and
V\asymp P^{\varepsilon}P^{\delta}Q/P=P^{1/3+\varepsilon+\delta}
. Therefore, for
\delta
sufficiently small (depending on
\varepsilon
and
s
) we have

\displaystyle E_{2,2}(P,\delta)

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}Y^{-s/2+1/2}Q^{-3/2}P^{s}(1+P^{2}W)^{-s/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times(Q^{1/4}(1+P^{2}W)^{1/4}+Q^{1/2}V^{-3/2})V^{s-1/2}

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}Y^{-s/2+1/2}Q^{-3/2}P^{s}(Q/Y)^{-s/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times(Q^{1/4}(Q/Y)^{1/4}+Q^{1/2}P^{-1/2})P^{s/3-1/6}

\displaystyle\ll P^{\varepsilon}\max_{\mathscr{Q}_{\delta}}Y^{-s/2+1/2}P^{s-2}(Q/Y)^{-s/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times(P^{1/3}(Q/Y)^{1/4}+P^{1/6})P^{s/3-1/6}.

Since the power of
Y
appearing in the above is positive, this contribution is maximum when
Y\asymp Q
and therefore for
\delta
small enough

\displaystyle E_{2,2}(P,\delta)

\displaystyle\ll Q^{-s/2+1/2}P^{s-2}(P^{1/3}+P^{1/6})P^{s/3-1/6+\varepsilon}

(13.16)

\displaystyle\ll P^{s-4-(s-9)/3-1/6+\varepsilon}.

Combining (
13.8
) (or (
13.9
)) and (
13.16
) we obtain Lemma
11.3
(
3
) when
s
is odd under GLH (or unconditionally, respectively).

14.
Heuristic comparison of delta symbol methods

In this section, we give a heuristic with which to compare our two-dimensional delta symbol to other existing methods.
Based on the one-dimensional delta symbol and the two-dimensional Farey dissection over function fields, we consider an
R
-dimensional delta symbol method over
\mathbb{Q}
to be any identity of the type

(14.1)

\delta_{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}=\sum_{1\leq q\leq Q}\ {\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{q}}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\leq\frac{Q^{\epsilon}}{qQ^{\eta}}\end{subarray}}p_{q,\vec{\mathrm{a}}}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})e((\vec{\mathrm{a}}/q+{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}+O_{\eta,N}(Q^{-N}),

for some
0<\eta\leq 1/R
, some explicit smooth functions
p_{q,\vec{\mathrm{a}}}
, and for all
\vec{\mathrm{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{R}
and all
Q,N,\epsilon>0
.
We believe that many practitioners of the circle method have some intuition along the following lines.

Heuristic 1
.

If we apply (
14.1
) to a sequence of vectors
\vec{\mathrm{n}}
which typically have size around
M
, then we should choose
Q
so that
M=Q^{1+\eta}
holds. In this way, for
q\asymp Q
, the function
e((\vec{\mathrm{a}}/q+{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})
does not oscillate very much as
{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}
varies over the domain of integration. It is desirable for the efficacy of this approach that
Q
should be taken as small as possible.

According to the heuristic above, it may be desired for
Q=M^{1/(1+\eta)}
to be as small as possible, in other words
\eta
should be as large as possible.
However we have the restriction
\eta\leq 1/R
in (
14.1
). The reason is that
the numbers
\vec{\mathrm{a}}/q+{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}
will have to run over essentially the entire unit box
[0,1]^{R}
in order to approximate the function
\delta_{\vec{\mathrm{n}}}
accurately in
\ell^{\infty}
norm. By Khinchine’s theorem, this requires
|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\gg_{\epsilon}q^{-1}Q^{-1/R-\epsilon}
for any
\epsilon>0
, and hence
\eta\leq 1/R
must hold.
In particular, our form of the two-dimensional delta symbol in Theorem
1.2
with
\eta=1/2
has the optimal choice of
Q=M^{2/3}
.

To perform a Kloosterman refinement by taking advantage of cancellations in the sum over
\vec{\mathrm{a}}
, one needs to arrange that the function
p_{q,\vec{\mathrm{a}}}
is the same for many different
\vec{\mathrm{a}}
. We partition
(\mathbb{Z}^{R}/q\mathbb{Z}^{R})^{*}
into equivalence classes
[\vec{\mathrm{a}}]
such that
[\vec{\mathrm{a}}]=[\vec{\mathrm{b}}]
exactly when
p_{q,\vec{\mathrm{a}}}=p_{q,\vec{\mathrm{b}}}
as functions. We can hope to make use of cancellations in the sums
\sum_{\vec{\mathrm{b}}\in[\vec{\mathrm{a}}]}e((\vec{\mathrm{b}}/q+{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})\cdot\vec{\mathrm{n}})
to get additional savings.
There may be a trivial class
\{\vec{\mathrm{a}}:p_{q,\vec{\mathrm{a}}}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})=0\ \forall{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\}
on which
p_{q,\vec{\mathrm{a}}}
vanishes; we shall exclude this class from our analysis as it contributes nothing to (
14.1
). We let
A
be the average size of a nontrivial class
[\vec{\mathrm{a}}]
, that is

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\Big(\sum_{1\leq q\leq Q}\#\mathscr{A}_{q}\Big)^{-1}\Big(\sum_{1\leq q\leq Q}\sum_{C\in\mathscr{A}_{q}}\#C\Big),

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{q}

\displaystyle=\{C:C=[\vec{\mathrm{a}}]\text{ for some }\vec{\mathrm{a}}\text{ with }(\vec{\mathrm{a}},q)=1,p_{q,\vec{\mathrm{a}}}\neq 0\}.

Our Theorem
1.2
allows averages over
\vec{\mathrm{a}}
with
A\asymp Q
.
We mention that the methods in
[
30
,
16
,
32
]
effectively take the optimal values
\eta=1/R
,
A=\#((\mathbb{Z}^{R}/q\mathbb{Z}^{R})^{*})
, but they can only be applied to situations where the exponential sum is an absolute square, due to the use of a classical major-arc/minor-arc decomposition rather than the
\delta
-method.
With this notation we give a heuristic for
\delta
-methods in Diophantine problems.

Heuristic 2
.

Suppose we use (
14.1
) as a form of the circle method to count solutions to
{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}=\vec{0}
, where
{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x})=(F_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\dots,F_{R}(\mathbf{x}))
is a system of
R
polynomials in
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}
with
|{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x})|\ll M
. Then we should take
Q=M^{1/(1+\eta)}
and the efficacy of (
14.1
) is roughly captured by the quantity

Q^{s/2}A^{-1/2},

where
A
is as defined above. A smaller value of this quantity suggests a more effective
\delta
-method. In principle, a double Kloosterman refinement utilizing also the average over
q
might save a another factor of
Q^{1/2}
, although in practice, this saving may be less due to lack of good estimates for short character sums without GLH. Additionally one may want to apply differencing methods to exponential sums over
{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}
, such as van der Corput differencing. In such cases one can instead take
M
to be an upper bound for a system
\Delta_{\mathbf{h}}{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x})
of differenced polynomials.

The reasoning behind Heuristic
2
is as follows. Using the delta symbol in (
14.1
) and summing over
\mathbf{x}
, we see that the number of
\mathbf{x}
with
|\mathbf{x}|\leq P,

{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}=\vec{0}
, is essentially given by

\sum_{q\leq Q}\underset{\hphantom{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{q}}}{\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{q}}}\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\ll\frac{1}{qQ^{\eta}}}p_{q,\vec{\mathrm{a}}}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})\sum_{|{\vec{\mathrm{x}}}|\leq P}e((\vec{\mathrm{a}}/q+{\vec{\mathrm{w}}})\cdot{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathbf{x}))d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}.

After any differencing steps one applies Poisson summation in the
\mathbf{x}
variable to modulus
q
; the innermost sum becomes
\sum_{\mathbf{u}}q^{-s}S_{q}(\vec{\mathrm{a}},\mathbf{u})I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})
for some exponential sums
S_{q}(\vec{\mathrm{a}},\mathbf{u})
and exponential integrals
I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})
. The integral
I_{q}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}},\mathbf{u})
allows a truncation of the
\mathbf{u}
variables up to essentially
\frac{q}{P}(1+M|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|)
(see e.g. Lemma
6.1
in our setting). The zero frequency
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{0}
will generally give the main term. For the non-zero frequencies, one may expect
I_{q}\ll P^{s}(1+M|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|)^{-s/2}
from stationary phase analysis and the bound
S_{q}\ll q^{s/2}
, assuming square-root cancellation of exponential sums. If we can further make use of the average over
\vec{\mathrm{a}}
to get a saving of
(\#[\vec{\mathrm{a}}])^{-1/2}
, we may find that the non-zero frequencies contribute to a term of size roughly

q^{s/2}(1+M|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|)^{s/2}(\#[\vec{\mathrm{a}}])^{-1/2}\leq(Q+M/Q^{\eta})^{s/2}(\#[\vec{\mathrm{a}}])^{-1/2}.

This error term is of size
\gg Q^{s/2}(\#[\vec{\mathrm{a}}])^{-1/2}
as soon as
Q\geq M^{1/(1+\eta)}\gg M^{R/(R+1)}
. This predicts an error in our original problem of size

\sum_{q\leq Q}\ {\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}\bmod{q}}}\int_{|{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}|\ll\frac{1}{qQ^{\eta}}}p_{q,\vec{\mathrm{a}}}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})O(Q^{s/2}(\#[\vec{\mathrm{a}}])^{-1/2})\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}.

We generally expect
\sum_{q}\sum_{\vec{\mathrm{a}}}^{*}\int|p_{q,\vec{\mathrm{a}}}({\vec{\mathrm{w}}})|\,d{\vec{\mathrm{w}}}\ll Q^{\epsilon}
to hold and that we can replace
\#[\vec{\mathrm{a}}]
by its average
A
. Hence the quantity above should be around
Q^{s/2}A^{-1/2}
as we posit above.
Furthermore, if one caries out a double Kloosterman refinement, one may save another
Q^{1/2}
from the
q
-sum.

Remark 14.1
.

It is tempting to interpret Heuristic
2
as a genuine prediction for the size of the error term in the best possible case.
But we should remember that the differencing procedures referred to at the end of the heuristic might alter the actual error term. Moreover better than square-root cancellation in exponential sums may be available such as
Ramanujan sums, which appear for quadratic forms in an even number of variables.

We now compare various versions of delta symbols in dimension two in applications to a pair of quadratic forms with the this heuristic, that is
R=2
and
{\vec{\mathrm{F}}}=(F_{1},F_{2})
are quadratic forms so that
M=P^{2}
.

Comparison 1
.

The nested delta symbol of Munshi
[
29
]
uses a value of
Q\asymp P^{3/2}
with a function
p_{q,\vec{\mathrm{a}}}=p_{q}
that allows an average over
\vec{\mathrm{a}}
with
A\asymp Q^{5/3}
. The value
Q^{5/3}
is drawn from equation (7) of Munshi’s paper, where
q_{1}q_{2}
different values of
\vec{\mathrm{a}}
are averaged with
q_{1}\leq P,q_{2}\leq P^{1/2}
and the additional condition that
q_{1}
divides a certain quadratic form
Q_{2}
. This congruence condition can be detected using additive characters modulo
q_{1}
, resulting in
q_{1}^{2}q_{2}
values of
\vec{\mathrm{a}}
, which is typically around
P^{5/2}=Q^{5/3}
values of
\vec{\mathrm{a}}
as claimed. Therefore, the heuristic suggests that (without introducing a double Kloosterman refinement) we have an error of size
Q^{s/2}A^{-1/2}\asymp Q^{s/2-5/6}
which is
P^{(3s-5)/4}
.

Our Theorem
1.2
allows us to take
Q=P^{4/3}
and to make use of
\vec{\mathrm{a}}
-averages with
A\asymp Q
. Thus the heuristic leads to an error term of size
Q^{s/2}A^{-1/2}=P^{2(s-1)/3}
, which is strictly smaller than the heuristic error term
P^{(3s-5)/4}
from Munshi
[
29
]
as soon as
s\geq 8
.
It is possible that further savings can be obtained as indicated in Remark
11.1
.

Both the nested
\delta
-method of Munshi and our Theorem
1.2
allow a double Kloosterman refinement, that is to make use of averages over
q
. In
Munshi
[
29
]
, any non-trivial bounds for character sums in the form
\sum_{q_{2}\leq P^{1/2}}\chi(q_{2})
for Dirichlet characters
\chi
with conductors up to
P
would suffice to handle the case
s=11
, which is the one he considers there. In our case, we need to have more than
Q^{-1/4}
in savings from the sum
\sum_{q\leq Q}\chi(q)
for Dirichlet characters
\chi
with conductor of size as large as
Q^{s/2}
to handle the case when
s=9
. Due to the large size of the conductors of the characters, this saving in the
q
-sum is more difficult to obtain unconditionally compared to that in Munshi
[
29
]
. In both methods, when
s
is even, one has more than square-root cancellations due to Ramanujan sums when the moduli
q
are generic, although typically this advantage is balanced against worse bounds for non-generic moduli.

According to Heuristic
2
, the smaller size of
Q
should be considerably more advantageous for equations of more variables or higher degrees, for which
M
is larger compared to the size of the variables. These will however bring additional complications by comparison with the quadratic case.

Comparison 2
.

Another point of comparison is the one-dimensional delta symbol method over
\mathbb{Q}(i)
, applied to the complex number
n=F_{1}+iF_{2}
, where
F_{i}
are quadratic forms in variable of size
P
. In the Gaussian integer version of the method, the denominators
q
would be Gaussian integers
q_{1}+iq_{2}
of absolute value at most
P
. However, to apply this to a pair
F_{1},F_{2}
with coefficients and variables in
\mathbb{Z}
, we must clear denominators to give rational vectors
\vec{\mathrm{a}}/\operatorname{Nm}(q)
, where the denominator now has size
P^{2}
. Thus in (
14.1
) we must actually take
Q=P^{2}
. We have
A\asymp q^{2}
, and so
Q^{s/2}A^{-1/2}\asymp P^{s-2}
. However, this heuristic is not accurate because, for sums that come from problems over
\mathbb{Q}[i]
, one should not perform Poisson summation modulo
\operatorname{Nm}(q)
but rather modulo
q
. This allows Browning–Pierce–Schindler
[
8
]
to obtain an error term smaller than
P^{s-4}
by this technique for certain pairs of quadratic forms.
